#5964. Where Is U.S. Security Assistance to Jordan Going?

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Where Is U.S. Security Assistance to Jordan Going?

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

The unprecedented terrorist attack on a church in Jordan last month that killed nine people and injured at least 34 has raised understandable concern about the spread of Salafist violence targeting civilians in the country. It should raise even greater concern about how Jordan’s government has used billions in U.S. security assistance and more than a decade of support for countering violent extremism.

The main perpetrator of the attack had only recently left prison following a terrorism conviction, yet the government has offered little explanation of how he went on to carry out the massacre. Instead, it launched kneejerk airstrikes on alleged Islamic State hideouts in Syria, killing seven civilians, three of them children, according to Syrian authorities. Both the failure to prevent the attack and the deadly retaliation expose a broader problem: security agencies empowered to raid, arrest, and punish, with little public accounting of whether their methods actually protect people.

The unprecedented terrorist attack on a church in Jordan last month that killed nine people and injured at least 34 has raised understandable concern about the spread of Salafist violence targeting civilians in the country. It should raise even greater concern about how Jordan’s government has used billions in U.S. security assistance and more than a decade of support for countering violent extremism.

The main perpetrator of the attack had only recently left prison following a terrorism conviction, yet the government has offered little explanation of how he went on to carry out the massacre. Instead, it launched kneejerk airstrikes on alleged Islamic State hideouts in Syria, killing seven civilians, three of them children, according to Syrian authorities. Both the failure to prevent the attack and the deadly retaliation expose a broader problem: security agencies empowered to raid, arrest, and punish, with little public accounting of whether their methods actually protect people.

The sequence of events is revealing. On Oct. 23, three gunmen—brothers Hazza Mohammad and Mishal Mohammad and their brother-in-law Ahmad Sami Hussein—attacked mourners at a funeral. The event was taking place at St. George’s Greek Orthodox Church in Hmoud, a small village in the Karak governorate of southern Jordan; the gunmen lived in a nearby village.

Karak itself is home to one of Jordan’s oldest Christian communities, dating back more than 1,500 years, and it has long been a model of Muslim-Christian cohesion. While there have been at least 12 attacks by Jordanian violent extremists in the past decade, all but one have targeted military and police sites. This attack was one of a very few in Jordan’s history to deliberately target Christians, who make up a mere 2.1 percent of the country’s population.

Following a three-day search, authorities captured the three alleged perpetrators on Oct. 26, wounding them, one critically, in a shoot-out at an abandoned house in Karak, where they also recovered weapons and substantial ammunition. The government initially claimed that the church attack was random and opportunistic, but it later revised its claim to confirm that this was a planned attack, documenting preparations involving smuggled weapons, vehicles, assigned roles, and escape routes.

The most damning facts to emerge, however, concern the authorities’ prior knowledge of Hazza Mohammad, the reported ringleader, and the police presence at the church before the attack. Jordanian authorities had released Hazza from prison less than six months ago, following the completion of a seven-year sentence for planning a terrorist attack and promoting Islamic State ideology. He presumably would have, or should have, remained under close surveillance by Jordanian authorities. On Oct. 26, a local woman reported that a police patrol was stationed at the church during Sunday worship services earlier this month, something that she had never seen before; a Karak churchgoer confirmed this in an Oct. 28 interview with the authors.

On the same night as the arrests of the three men, the Jordanian government launched an aerial bombardment on four rural locations near Daraa and Suwayda in Syria. The attack is confirmed to have killed five family members in al-Tuff, three of them children; an 80-year-old man at a farm in al-Shiyah; and a 60-year-old man in Shahib. Local reporting suggested that the Jordanian Armed Forces appear to have been aware that women and children were at al-Tuff but hit it anyway.

The government claimed that it had identified Islamic State cell hideouts connected to the attackers but presented no evidence establishing that connection. It said Hazza had called a number in Daraa shortly before the attack but did not establish whom he contacted or how that call connected the Syrian targets to the church shooting. Jordanian Interior Minister Mazen al-Faraya said the attackers shared the same ideology as the Islamic State and al Qaeda but acknowledged that investigators had not established whether they belonged to, or were influenced by, either organization.

The Jordanian government acknowledged the civilian deaths that it caused and subsequently offered condolences to the victims, though not compensation. It insisted that it was targeting terrorists and that its strike had killed 20 Islamic State members and destroyed a weapons depot and some vehicles. To date, however, there has been no independent verification of any Islamic State deaths; Syrian rescuers at the site reported only the recovery of seven civilian bodies.

The Jordanian government also claimed that it had coordinated the attack with the Syrian government, but Damascus denied this, condemned the breach of Syrian airspace—a clear violation of sovereignty—and summoned Jordan’s ambassador to deliver a formal protest. The two governments subsequently agreed to a joint security committee to establish the facts.

Jordan’s security failures, both before and after the attack, come despite years of foreign investment in its security apparatus—and at a moment when Americans are turning away from foreign military aid. U.S. President Donald Trump announced in early November that billions in annual aid to Israel would be terminated; this would directly impact the military aid the United States allocates to Jordan and Egypt as their “peace dividend” for entering into peace agreements with Israel.

As part of this understanding, the U.S. government has provided almost $5 billion in military assistance to Jordan since 2015, alongside substantial economic assistance. Public records document hundreds of millions of dollars in surveillance-related programs, including more than $234 million between 2009-2021 for the Jordan Border Security Program and additional assistance for intelligence and surveillance.

Separately, public reporting establishes that the CIA funds Jordan’s General Intelligence Directorate, which it helped establish, though it provides no public information about the amount of its support. European governments, the European Union, and Canada also have granted roughly $200 million since 2016 to support Jordan’s policing, intelligence gathering, surveillance, border security, and military.

However, the Jordanian government has provided no meaningful accounting about how it has used these generous foreign resources to protect its citizens, particularly from terrorist violence. Its “Countering Violent Extremism” (CVE) unit, funded by the State Department and the European Union, offers almost no information about its activities, its budget, or its staff, despite its 11 years in operation.

Established in 2015 under the Ministry of the Interior, the program was bounced to the Ministry of Culture in 2016 and then to the prime minister’s office in 2020. The prime ministry’s website links the unit to a 2018 report, while a recent official news item describes its director attending a conference in Japan.

Even the U.S. State Department issued a rare rebuke about Jordan’s unit in a 2023 report, citing the government’s lack of a “sustained level of coordination or responsiveness… for CVE efforts,” and noting the “numerous Jordanian government stakeholders with equities in PVE [preventing violent extremism], making strategic collaboration by the donor community challenging.”

What little we do know about Jordan’s CVE effort is that it has relied extensively on a heavy-handed security approach while neglecting the economic, political, and social grievances that researchers consider essential to address in countering radicalization. A comprehensive 2025 study of Jordan’s response to violent extremism found extensive reliance on preventive raids and arrests and abuse of overbroad anti-terrorism laws. It also documented overlapping military, police, and intelligence operations with little transparency as well as broad intelligence agency veto powers over civil society prevention programs.

This same security apparatus has instead prioritized shielding the monarchy from political dissent. Over the past five years, the government has carried out mass spying and arrests of teachers, doctors, bus drivers, and supporters of Palestine who have protested lack of democracy and economic injustice in the country, “preemptively” arresting those merely planning to attend protests, shuttering the teacher’s union, prosecuting the leadership of the country’s human rights body, dissolving 19 political parties, and jailing members of the country’s now-banned Muslim Brotherhood organization.

Jordan needs to come clean about how a previously convicted extremist carried out an attack just after his release, what rehabilitation he received, and how authorities assessed and monitored his risk after release. It must also produce the evidence that it relied on to justify its bungled strike in Syria and identify the security reforms that it will make to protect Jordanians, in particular its religious minorities.

Such accountability will require broader democratic reform to make security agencies answerable to the citizens that they are supposed to protect, rather than the monarchy.

Sarah Leah Whitson is a lawyer and a co-author of the recently published From Apartheid to Democracy: A Blueprint for Peace in Israel-Palestine. She previously served as the executive director of DAWN as well as Human Rights Watch’s Middle East and North Africa Division. X: @sarahleah1

Jamal Al-Tahat is a Jordanian analyst. He previously served as a senior advisor at DAWN.

#5963. The Missing Critical Minerals Investment

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Tom Moerenhout is a Professor at Columbia University’s School of Public and International Affairs, Director of Critical Materials at Columbia University’s Center on Global Energy Policy, and Executive Director at the Geneva Platform for Resilient Value Chains.

Kevin Brunelli is a Nonresident Fellow at Columbia University’s Center on Global Energy Policy and previously served in the Office of Critical Minerals and Energy Supply Chains at the U.S. Department of State.

To Catch Up to China, America and Its Allies Must Dig

The United States and its allies are finally taking supply chains for critical minerals seriously. China has spent the last three decades building a dominant share of global mineral production, and the export controls Beijing has introduced periodically since 2023 have made clear just how dangerous it is for other countries to rely on China for access to materials that are essential components of semiconductors, defense technologies, and automobiles.

Washington and European capitals are now trying to minimize their exposure. Alongside private firms, these governments have begun investing in both upstream industries including mines, refineries, and recycling infrastructure and downstream industries such as magnets and batteries. The bulk of their efforts so far have focused on mines already in operation or close to it and on refining. It is true that the United States and Europe do not have sufficient facilities to process raw ore into specialized chemicals or refined materials and that building more refining facilities will help reduce their reliance on China and alleviate immediate supply chain concerns.

But this is a temporary fix. Refining facilities are valuable only if they have a steady, long-term supply of raw materials. That, in turn, requires new mines, especially as advanced industries demand ever more minerals. Because building a mine takes roughly 20 years, ensuring a long-term supply of minerals requires investing in new mines now. China, which is committed to maintaining its supply chain dominance, has been willing to allocate substantial capital to such ventures. Japan has, too, ever since China blocked exports of critical minerals to the country amid a dispute over the Senkaku Islands (known in China as the Diaoyu Islands) in 2010, which pushed Tokyo to start the long process of disentangling its own supply chains. But Japan still has a long way to go; after 15 years of investing, the country has reduced its dependence on China for rare earths from about 90 percent to between 65 and 70 percent—not nearly enough to supply its own needs, much less those of its allies.

In short, investing in refining is necessary but insufficient. To avoid future supply chain vulnerabilities, the United States and its allies, especially those in the G-7, must also start building a pipeline of new mines. These investments are long bets, and they are risky. But when successful, they reap high rewards.

The United States and its allies significantly outperform China in mineral exploration. Australian and Canadian firms alone account for roughly one-third of global exploration spending. But the vast majority of geological prospects never become operating mines. This is not for lack of financing in prospecting but because of the many financial disincentives and risks that appear once a mining project moves forward.

The initial stages of mining development rely chiefly on a unique ecosystem of mining companies called “juniors,” so called because they have a small market cap, focus exclusively on one or a few projects, and are nowhere large enough to list on major stock exchanges. Most of them list in Australia or Canada and concentrate on the initial high-risk exploration stages. Geological uncertainty makes betting on junior ventures extraordinarily risky, but if a mineral deposit proves to have significant reserves, the junior can sell the project or bring in a new partner, and that payout is large.

Yet in the next stages, investors tend to lose patience or run out of capital. Although initial prospecting is relatively cheap, subsequent feasibility studies that estimate a deposit’s size, engineering needs, costs, and expected returns can easily run into the tens of millions of dollars, in addition to the costs of drilling, engineering, permitting, and development planning. Commercial lenders stay away because these projects have no immediate revenue, no assets to offer as collateral, and no guarantees they will even be viable. The risk, in short, is too high to take for uncertain returns. Large mining companies also avoid these projects because they face pressure from shareholders to provide maximum returns. Even as demand for minerals has risen, the share of revenue that major mining companies have reinvested in new development has stayed flat.

Without outside financing, juniors cannot complete the studies needed to attract additional financing for construction, even if they have found a promising deposit. As single-asset companies, they have no other cash flow or assets to offer as collateral for additional loans. This moment is often referred to as the “valley of death.” Many companies looking for a way out of this predicament have turned to one reliable source of investment: China. Chinese companies are much more willing to take on this type of risk and provide the necessary capital to advance mining projects, and they are often the only viable option for juniors. Thus, China’s dominance in global supply chains relies on Western companies’ exploration. It endures not because China offers more competitive terms but because at a key financing stage, it is the only game in town.

China has been expanding its supply pipeline for critical minerals for decades. In 2025, it spent a record $32 billion on metals and mining projects through its Belt and Road Initiative, breaking the previous year’s record of $21 billion. Chinese firms are also willing to invest in equity, buying ownership stakes in companies that come with no guaranteed returns. Additional spending takes the form of resources-for-infrastructure deals, by which China funds infrastructure such as ports, energy facilities, and railways in mineral-rich countries in exchange for a share in the profits of mining projects—which Chinese companies and state banks may also help finance. By investing in a broad portfolio of mineral projects across several countries, Chinese firms can let the profits of those that succeed cover the losses of those that fail.

Apart from capital, the most important part of China’s mining strategy is know-how. Because failure is so common, the ability to identify deposits with the highest potential to deliver is crucial. Beijing relies on specialized state-owned enterprises, geological firms, private companies, and government officials to vet projects and work with host governments to secure permits, infrastructure, and delivery. The only G-7 country that maintains a comparable on-the-ground presence for mining projects is Japan, in countries such as Australia, Canada, Peru, and Uzbekistan. These projects are intended to secure critical minerals that can support Japan’s industries, but they are currently insufficient to meet all of Japan’s production needs, let alone broader allied needs.

The disparity between China’s mining investments and those of the United States and its allies is most evident in Africa, home to some of the largest untapped mineral resources. Whereas the United States invested about $300 million in critical mineral projects on the continent in 2023, China invested about $8 billion to $10 billion. Washington has recognized the need to close this gap, but it still has a long way to go.

In the Democratic Republic of the Congo, a particular focus of the Trump administration, Chinese companies control about 70 percent of cobalt and copper mines. Between 2008 and 2020, Chinese banks and companies extended over $9 billion in credit for mine development and operations in Congo, in addition to $3 billion in infrastructure loans from the Export-Import Bank of China. The United States and other G-7 countries had the opportunity to take part in these investments but judged the projects to be too risky. In 2015, for example, the Canadian-listed mining company Ivanhoe Mines sought funding for its Kamoa-Kakula copper project in Congo. With no Western investors willing to take on the risk, the Chinese firm Zijin Mining acquired an ownership stake. Today, Kamoa-Kakula is on track to become one of the largest copper mines in the world. Meanwhile, in 2016 and 2020, the only major American mining company active in Congo sold its stakes in two substantial copper-cobalt deposits, its only remaining stakes in the country, to a Chinese mining company.

The owners of a mine control where its minerals eventually go. When Chinese firms own a mine, the output flows either to their refineries or downstream manufacturers in China. The Chinese battery giant CATL, for example, has an ownership stake in the Kisanfu mine in Congo, where it secures a steady supply of copper and cobalt for its batteries. U.S. automakers and allied battery producers have also invested in some projects, but none to the same degree—even still, much of that material flows through China. The concentration of this refined material in China gives Beijing ever-greater market power and the ability to weaponize it.

In theory, the United States and its allies have the means of financing their own pipeline of future mining projects. In 2024, two Toronto-based stock exchanges hosted nearly 1,100 publicly listed mining companies, 40 percent of the global total. These companies account for roughly one-third of the world’s total mining equity capital raised over the last five years. About half of the listed companies’ projects were based in Canada. The Australian Stock Exchange is another key exchange, with about 850 companies listed in 2024 for mining development.

But these companies are facing pressures that discourage investment in promising projects. The world’s most accessible mineral deposits have already been tapped, so potential new deposits are often located in more complicated geological environments, making the early stages of exploration more difficult and costly. Moving from exploration to production is also taking more time, as increased social and environmental requirements draw out permitting processes. This lengthening timeline lowers private investors’ appetite for risk because they have to wait longer for their investments to start generating returns. At the same time, Chinese overproduction of critical minerals has depressed prices for key commodities. Despite strong signals that demand for the minerals will continue to increase, these low prices discourage new players from entering the game and weaken investors’ incentives to help those already in it.

U.S. and allied governments therefore need to draw in substantial new investment. The United States has made a start by taking equity stakes in domestic and international mining companies and expanding loans to them. Official announcements claim that these investments total around $50 billion, although the value of the deals that have been signed falls closer to $9 billion, and the money actually moving adds up to around $4 billion. Most of this money does not go to the projects languishing in the valley of death. Instead, the projects getting funded are those that are closer to production, because they can help tackle immediate supply risks.

This is a good start, but ventures that remain at early stages still need funding, and private investors are still wary of mining projects. Since 2025, the few institutional investors that have gotten involved tended to do so after the U.S. government announced an equity stake or loan to back a project. Although such caution is not new, relying on government signals and public investment sets up a perverse incentive for private companies not to invest in the absence of public support, which is exactly what governments want to avoid.

To really pull in private capital for exploration and mine development, the United States and the European Union should take a page from Canada’s book. The Canadian government offers tax credits to companies engaged in mineral exploration, and its “flow-through shares” system helps mining companies finance exploration and development by offering tax incentives to investors. If the rest of the G-7 can establish this kind of financial architecture, far more exploration capital would flow to critical mineral projects. And if member countries link their systems by letting investors claim tax benefits for exploration anywhere in the G-7, they could build the large, shared pool of exploration and development capital needed to support projects through to construction. In addition, G-7 countries must remove barriers for large institutional investors to enter the mining sector—primarily by reforming their tax codes, which currently disincentivize tax-exempt investors, such as pension funds, endowments, and foundations, from investing in mining projects because returns on such investments can be taxed as income.

Finally, when the incentives for private investment still do not line up for certain projects, G-7 countries should use public capital to directly help mining companies escape the valley of death. To do this, the United States, Japan, and European countries should set up a joint facility to pool financing for feasibility studies, which are particularly difficult to raise private funding for, establishing a broad portfolio so that the losses from any failures are covered by the winners. This pooled fund would allocate grants to promising projects in exchange for equity in the project or a royalty once the mine goes into production. Many projects would still fail, but with a dedicated, well-staffed facility, the quality of investments would likely improve—as they have for China, which takes a similar portfolio approach to early-stage mining. And the benefits to both investors and national interests would far outweigh the lost capital.

Today’s chokepoints for critical minerals sit mostly at the processing or refining stage, but future threats to the minerals needed for manufacturing, energy, artificial intelligence, and defense technology will be at the mines. G-7 countries have the markets and the policy tools to build a pipeline of secure mining projects and prevent the next economic emergency. Investment in refining facilities means little if the United States and its allies have no minerals to feed them a decade from now. The only choice, then, is to commit to financing new mining projects now.

#5961. Democrats rally behind GOP lawmaker's Hegseth impeachment push, signal more to come in January

Secretary of War Pete Hegseth delivers a defiant defense at the Pentagon as Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., pushes to introduce articles of impeachment against him. Fox News chief national security correspondent Jennifer Griffin reports on Hegseth’s response and the impeachment threat on ‘The Faulkner Focus.’

Top Democrats are lining up behind Republican Rep. Thomas Massie's effort to impeach War Secretary Pete Hegseth, signaling that the fight could continue when Democrats take control of Congress in January.

"Hard yes on the Hegseth impeachment resolution," House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., said ahead of its introduction.

The Democratic reactions online telegraph an appetite to scrutinize Trump’s administration through congressional power once their majorities are formally sworn in in January, at the start of the 120th Congress.

HAKEEM JEFFRIES DECLARES BID FOR HOUSE SPEAKER AFTER GOP'S MIDTERM MASSACRE

Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., speaks on impeaching Secretary of War Pete Hegseth outside the U.S. Capitol on Nov. 9, 2026, in Washington, D.C. (Andrew Harnik/Getty Images)

Massie himself framed the effort as likely to be repeated in the new year.

"My Hegseth impeachment resolution is a much gentler version of what’s coming when Dems control Congress," Massie said.

Already, Rep. Al Green, D-Texas — who has repeatedly introduced impeachment resolutions against President Donald Trump — urged his colleagues to expand their horizons for impeachment moments after Massie introduced his resolution.

"If the Constitution is to be honored and Congress respected, a president whom many members of Congress have declared impeachable must be impeached. Failure to do so would be another step toward making the Constitution meaningless and Congress powerless," Green wrote online.

Massie is filing eight articles of impeachment against Hegseth, accusing him of violating the War Powers Resolution in Iran and Yemen, failing to prevent civilian casualties, using lethal force in an extrajudicial manner, suppressing free speech and kidnapping a sovereign foreign leader without congressional authorization with the capture of Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro.

Although he didn’t mention Massie by name, Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., echoed Jeffries’ enthusiasm.

"The Senate needs to put Pete Hegseth on trial so we can remove him for his catastrophic failure in Iran," Schumer said in remarks on the Senate floor.

Even beyond a review of Hegseth’s record as war secretary, some Democrats argued that removing Hegseth would help end the conflict — a war that Democrats believe has extended far beyond Trump’s power to use the military in a conflict without a declaration of war from Congress.

"Impeach Hegseth. End the war," Rep. Ro Khanna, D-Calif., said in a post to X.

7 REPUBLICANS REBEL AGAINST TRUMP, VOTE WITH DEMS TO REIN IN IRAN WAR POWERS

Secretary of War Pete Hegseth speaks at the Association of the United States Army Annual Meeting and Exposition in Washington, D.C., on Oct. 14, 2026. (Jim Watson/AFP via Getty Images)

Calls for Hegseth’s impeachment have circulated among Democrats for months. Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., has frequently called for Hegseth to step down.

"It’s way past time," Omar, who shares her home state of Minnesota with Hegseth, said in September.

"He has made a mockery of our country; it risks our national security and it’s losing us respect."

On Monday afternoon, Omar doubled down on those calls, arguing that Hegseth had operated beyond the boundaries of his office in conducting strikes on boats off the coast of Venezuela for allegedly carrying narcotics.

"Pete Hegseth has grossly abused his position as Secretary of Defense. He is waging an illegal war in Iran, conducting extrajudicial killings in the Caribbean and continues to put innocent civilians in harm’s way," Omar said.

Rep. Gregory Meeks, D-N.Y., the ranking member of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, echoed Omar’s list of grievances.

"He has caused the military and the members therein to have the lowest morale we’ve seen by his actions — firing individuals just based upon just cultural issues, basically. That kind of thing is not who we want to be a representative of us in the United States defense, and I think he should resign. And if I have to vote on it, whether it’s today or tomorrow or next week, to say that he should be impeached, I will vote so," Meeks said on Tuesday.

When asked if Democrats should renew the effort in January should Massie’s resolution face defeat, Meeks hinted that he believed so.

GOP LAWMAKER SAYS MORE QUALIFIED OFFICIALS SHOULD LEAD PENTAGON, QUESTIONS HEGSETH'S 'MORAL COMPASS'

Secretary of War Pete Hegseth takes questions from reporters during a news conference at the Pentagon on Nov. 10, 2026, in Arlington, Virginia. (Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images)

"I’m not going to let it go," Meeks said. "We’re going to continue to dig into him, his involvement, his advice that led us to this illegal war in the first place."

The House must approve articles of impeachment, and the Senate must vote to convict Hegseth for him to be removed from office.

The resolution can clear the House with a simple majority but requires two-thirds of senators to affirm a guilty verdict.

Leo Briceno is a politics reporter for the congressional team at Fox News Digital. He was previously a reporter with World Magazine.

#5960. The Global Economy Braces for Yet Another Blow

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: The Global Economy Braces for Yet Another Blow

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

Deadly flooding sweeping the American Southwest. Unrelenting downpours clobbering South America. A punishing drought gripping Southeast Asia.

The world is still seeing the evolution of El Niño, the naturally occurring phenomenon that scrambles weather patterns worldwide and can wreak havoc on agricultural production. But these snapshots offer a window—and warning—for what could come as the full force of this powerful event crashes into a global economy that is already battered by the Iran war and months of trade turmoil.

Deadly flooding sweeping the American Southwest. Unrelenting downpours clobbering South America. A punishing drought gripping Southeast Asia.

The world is still seeing the evolution of El Niño, the naturally occurring phenomenon that scrambles weather patterns worldwide and can wreak havoc on agricultural production. But these snapshots offer a window—and warning—for what could come as the full force of this powerful event crashes into a global economy that is already battered by the Iran war and months of trade turmoil.

“The effects of these El Niños are not benign,” said Cullen Hendrix, a senior fellow at the Peterson Institute for International Economics who has estimated that this event could cost the global economy more than $7 trillion over the next five years. “We’re talking about a phenomenon that has systemic impacts that can adversely affect populations all across the globe.”

The world is no stranger to this phenomenon, which occurs about every two to seven years and can last for up to 18 months. But no two El Niños are identical.

“Each one has its own little fingerprint,” said Brian Barker, a principal faculty specialist at the University of Maryland who works for NASA Harvest, the agency’s food security and agriculture program. “Until we actually see it develop and come to fruition, we won’t know exactly what it’s going to be.”

And this year’s event is shaping into what could be the strongest El Niño ever recorded. The U.S. National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration has forecast an 83 percent likelihood that the world sees a strong-to-very strong El Niño in early 2027, and the World Meteorological Organization (WMO) has projected that the strength of this event could exceed the scale of its global monitoring charts.

Governments, banks, and humanitarian agencies are bracing for substantial disruptions to critical infrastructure, food production, and global inflation.

Impacts on agricultural yields are highly uneven: Southern Africa, Central America, India, and Australia are set to feel the sharpest pain, while the United States and Central Asia could see benefits. At the global level, crop production impacts typically offset each other, and ample inventories are expected to help cushion the shock.

Past El Niños have taken a profound toll. For example, the El Niño of 2015-16 throttled some 60 million people, triggering $5 billion in humanitarian appeals across nearly two dozen countries, according to the U.N. Food and Agriculture Organization. While estimates range, studies suggest that prior events cost at least tens of billions, or even trillions, of dollars in economic damage globally.

This El Niño is expected to reach its full might in December, but it is already leaving a trail of damage around the world. Peru and Bolivia recently declared states of emergency, with drought parching nearly 80,000 families in the latter, according to Bolivian officials. Indian farmers are grappling with the country’s weakest rainfall in a decade, while sweeping wildfires have smothered Indonesia.

The big problem is that many of these countries are still reeling from the economic and energy shock waves of the Iran war and have little cushion to absorb new pressures. Hendrix, the Peterson economist who estimated that the economic losses of this El Niño could balloon to more than $7 trillion, said that the costs will deal a particularly painful blow to lower- and middle-income countries.

The one-year losses associated with this El Niño are likely to scale to about a trillion dollars, he said. “To put that in ballpark terms, that’s roughly the equivalent of wiping an economy the size of Mexico off the face of the earth,” Hendrix said.

As meteorological warnings have grown more urgent, aid agencies have leapt into action, although their networks have been strained by funding cuts and aid reduction. Last month, the United Nations Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs (OCHA) and WMO launched an appeal for immediate assistance to assist regions already facing El Niño’s impacts.

Of particular concern is Sudan, which has been wracked by a devastating civil war and is now seeing intensified famine concerns amid El Niño’s resurgence and drought threat; 19.5 million people across the country face acute hunger, according to the U.N. World Food Program, while more than 4 million women and children are acutely malnourished. The organization is grappling with a $300 million funding shortfall to continue operations.

There is also a lag between the onset of drought and its effect on food security, meaning that some of the worst effects likely won’t be seen for months to come. With drought, the most severe impacts can be delayed by six to nine months, or even a year, according to Richard Choularton, the director of the World Food Program’s Climate and Resilience Service.

“This El Nino is the behemoth. It is a wrecking ball,” OCHA chief Tom Fletcher said at a press briefing. “It would be an extraordinary failure to see this crisis coming, to hear the predictions from the experts, and yet still squander the opportunity to protect people in its path.”

Christina Lu is a staff writer at Foreign Policy. Bluesky: @christinalu.bsky.social X: @christinafei

#5958. The Dangers of a Lame-Duck Trump

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

DANIEL W. DREZNER is Academic Dean and Distinguished Professor of International Politics at the Fletcher School of Law and Diplomacy at Tufts University and the author of the newsletter Drezner’s World.

ELIZABETH N. SAUNDERS is Professor of Political Science at Columbia University, a Nonresident Senior Fellow at the Brookings Institution, and the author of The Insiders’ Game: How Elites Make War and Peace.

How Far Will He Go to Make Peace—and War?

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

With the midterms over, U.S. President Donald Trump is now a lame duck. Second-term presidents who lose control of Congress have to face the reality that the time for signature legislation or other domestic policy initiatives has passed. As they think about their legacy, lame ducks often turn to foreign policy, where the power of the presidency allows them to act unfettered by pesky domestic politics. It is not unusual for a lame duck to reach for a final, lofty goal abroad. U.S. President Bill Clinton, for instance, put great effort into trying to advance a proposal for a final settlement to the Israeli-Palestinian conflict at the end of his presidency.

Trump’s lame-duck period will not be like those of his predecessors, however. He has already gone through what was widely considered at the time to be a lame-duck phase, between November 2020 and January 2021. He used it to contest the election outcome despite clear evidence that Joe Biden had won. Trump is the first president in the past 100 years to have served two nonconsecutive terms, and therefore the only American leader in living memory to have a second lame-duck period—this time lasting more than two years.

With Democrats soon to be in control of the House and the Senate, the threat of impeachment or investigations into the Trump family’s business dealings will likely make the president feel cornered and angry. And that’s what makes the remainder of Trump’s term so dangerous. A boxed-in Trump, with no obvious constructive foreign policy outlet to channel his energy, may well lash out internationally. And Congress, even if controlled by Democrats, will have little power over his day-to-day management of foreign policy.

There are plenty of ways Trump could act out: issuing fresh threats against Canada, launching new attacks on U.S. alliances, or colluding with Russia or China at the expense of Ukraine or Taiwan. But to turn those threats into policy would require serious effort, such as repositioning U.S. troops or painstakingly negotiating with other great powers.

The two places where Trump can inflict the most damage are Iran and Cuba. According to The New York Times, the Trump administration and Israel have drawn up plans for fresh strikes on Iran as soon as the midterms are over. A last-ditch effort to compel Tehran to surrender is unlikely to work and would put at risk the global economy and the security and stability of Persian Gulf allies. Trump might also try to force regime change in Cuba. Military action against both adversaries would only require Trump to give his say-so. With no election on the horizon, all that constrains the president abroad is the sense of his own legacy.

We define presidents as lame-duck when they no longer need to contend with elections, either presidential or midterm. For a one-term president who has lost a reelection bid, this period lasts just a few months, between their successor’s election and inauguration. For a second-term president, it can last two years, between the midterms and when they leave office. (Trump, who has never been particularly interested in the political fortunes of other Republicans, is unlikely to let the 2028 presidential election affect his thinking.)

Freed from the burdens of campaigning and legislating, and often lacking domestic political capital, lame-duck leaders tend to pivot to diplomacy, taking chances abroad that they would not have dared to before. Clinton tried to broker an Israeli-Palestinian peace agreement and arrange a presidential trip to North Korea at the end of his second term. After losing the 2014 midterms, President Barack Obama concluded the Iran nuclear deal and normalized relations with Cuba.

Lame-duck presidents have also used military force in ways they might not have done in the lead-up to elections. George H. W. Bush, for example, intervened in Somalia in late 1992 with a limited mission to ease the famine. More than a decade later, in 2007, George W. Bush sent a surge of forces to stabilize Iraq after a punishing midterm election in which voters seemed to send the message that he should withdraw from the failing war there.

The results of these foreign policy moonshots have been mixed. Clinton’s Israeli-Palestinian negotiations failed; Obama’s diplomatic wins were short-lived; George H. W. Bush’s Somalia mission was largely successful in staving off hunger but left the roots of the conflict unresolved, tempting Clinton to expand the mission with disastrous consequences; and George W. Bush’s Iraq surge quelled violence in the short term but did little to permanently stabilize the country. These endeavors were, at the very least, debated and planned with input from relevant parts of government and outside experts. Their aims were clear, even if unmet. And in each case the president attempted to explain and justify his policies—standards Trump has routinely failed to meet.

With Trump now diminished by the midterms yet still on a quest to win a Nobel Peace Prize, one might expect him to turn to diplomacy. The problem is that he has already exhausted many of the obvious lame-duck gambits. During his first term, Trump swung for the foreign policy fences by pulling out of the 2015 Iran nuclear deal, meeting North Korean leader Kim Jong Un three times, replacing the North American Free Trade Agreement with his own regional trade pact, facilitating the normalization of ties between Israel and a handful of Arab countries, and withdrawing from a raft of international agreements. Since returning to office in 2025, he has presided over a cease-fire in Gaza and the capture of Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro.

Trump is not expected to return to most of these initiatives because he has reached the limits of what his spectacle-driven diplomacy can achieve. Trump vowed to take Greenland, for example, but has settled for a deal that expands the U.S. military presence in the territory. His “Board of Peace,” which he pitched as a rival to the United Nations Security Council, will continue to languish as other countries now feel less need to kowtow to his every whim.

There are other long shots Trump could attempt, such as trying to end intractable conflicts in Kashmir or Sudan, to forge a reputation as a peacemaker. But these are exceptionally difficult problems to solve in the best of circumstances. Even forcing a lopsided deal in these cases would require a level of deftness, regional expertise, and patience that Trump lacks. He rarely invests meaningful time in diplomacy, as illustrated by his dubious claims to have ended a slew of conflicts, many of which are still ongoing.

Perhaps the biggest swing Trump could take would be to fashion a great-power concert—a forum in which he, Russian President Vladimir Putin, and Chinese leader Xi Jinping worked together to impose global order. As with most of his prior meetings with Putin and Xi, however, future summits are unlikely to produce anything of substance. Neither Russia nor China has expressed much interest in the trilateral nuclear arms treaty that Trump has floated. Nor are they keen on pressing Iran to acquiesce to a deal with the United States.

Trump could use those meetings to throw Ukraine or Taiwan under the bus in the desperate hope of a peace deal. But as the past two years have demonstrated, Putin is not interested in compromise and has the resources to keep fighting. Xi views Taiwan as a domestic Chinese matter and will be unlikely to accept any constraints on his approach to the island. Trump will eventually figure out that he’s better off capitalizing on the optics of a summit of the three leaders than putting in the work to reach any kind of agreement.

Trump is most likely to take action in Iran. He seems to believe that escalating the war could break the stalemate and rescue his legacy. But Iranian leaders won’t back down in the face of more force. Instead, they will probably ramp up attacks on commercial shipping, strike Gulf allies, and ignore diplomatic overtures until Trump leaves office. Moreover, Trump’s own options may be limited: a more radical U.S. escalation against Iran, such as deploying troops to Kharg Island, would probably be so unpopular within his base that he would avoid it. Even Trump’s biggest supporters are growing tired of the high prices that his war has caused. Renewed conflict could plunge the world into economic crisis.

Trump could also attempt to force regime change in Cuba. He has imposed a full-blown embargo on the island for most of 2026 in an effort to weaken its defenses. Cut off from its Venezuelan lifeline and without electricity for all but a few hours a day, the economic hardships and human suffering in Cuba have dramatically worsened in recent months. Ousting Cuba’s government is a personal priority of Marco Rubio, Trump’s secretary of state and national security adviser, and would align with the “Donroe Doctrine,” an assertion of U.S. primacy in the Western Hemisphere.

For Trump, an attack on Cuba would be attractive because it would not require too much in the way of military assets and, at least in his mind, would solve a problem that has bedeviled U.S. presidents stretching back to the end of the Eisenhower administration. Trump might have already moved on Cuba had he not gotten bogged down in Iran. Of course, as the political scientist Alexander Downes has shown, using force to achieve regime change rarely works and often leads to unpredictable consequences—in the case of Cuba, it might mean a new influx of refugees to the United States.

But a lame-duck Trump could see it as a gamble worth taking on the off chance that he can be the president to finally unseat the communist regime. When it comes to foreign policy, Trump loves nothing more than attempting to hack open a Gordian knot. He might also view regime change in Cuba as a way to distract from his failure in Iran.

Another possibility is that Trump will focus his policy efforts abroad on enriching his family. He has already created ample opportunities for foreign entities to seemingly buy influence in his administration. Trump and his family members have accepted a jetliner from the emir of Qatar, a $2 billion investment from a fund led by the Saudi crown prince, and profits from a number of cryptocurrency enterprises, much of which came from foreign buyers. According to The New Yorker, Trump’s family has reaped $4 billion through such profiteering. Trump, secure in the knowledge that the Senate will not have the 67 votes it needs to remove him from office, might figure that he has nothing to lose in pursuing more moneymaking schemes.

The most surprising foreign policy option Trump could take during his lame-duck period, however, is doing nothing at all. The president has busied himself with remaking Washington, D.C., in his own image by redecorating the Oval Office, designing a White House ballroom, and attempting to remodel the Kennedy Center. There is a chance that he could spend his last two years in office issuing pardons, trying to prosecute political enemies on the side, and erecting monuments to himself. Given the alternatives, inaction on foreign policy may well be the best-case scenario for Trump’s lame-duck years.

American Foreign Policy in an Age of Unrestrained Executive Power

The Unconstrained Presidency and the End of American Primacy

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

How Far Will He Go to Make Peace—and War?

What the Godfather of Realism Can Teach Trump

Beijing’s Theory of Success in the Face of Failure

#5957. Trump's first Iran war escalation post-midterm losses seeks to choke off Tehran’s trade

Farzin Nadimi explains how the U.S. could target Iran’s remaining land routes — and why sealing them off will be a "mid- to long-term project."

President Donald Trump isn't backing off his pressure campaign against Iran despite claims the conflict contributed to sweeping Republican losses in last week's midterm elections.

In fact, Trump is doubling down by expanding U.S. efforts to choke off the trade routes Tehran increasingly relies on as it tries to rebuild its military in the midst of war.

The new effort, dubbed Task Force Iron Gate, takes aim at a vulnerability that has persisted despite the U.S. naval blockade: the land and Caspian Sea routes Iran can use to bring in goods, dual-use technology and military supplies beyond the reach of American warships.

That approach, one expert claims, fits into a broader administration strategy of tightening the squeeze on Tehran while stopping short of restarting full-scale military operations. Meanwhile, others caution that it could produce escalation rather than an immediate diplomatic breakthrough.

IRAN VOWS ‘ASTONISHING’ STRIKES AND LIFTS MISSILE-RANGE LIMIT AS US TARGETS LAND ROUTES

An Iranian cargo truck crosses into the Pakistan-Iran border as an Iranian national flag (L) flies at half-mast at Taftan, Balochistan province on March 1, 2026 after the death of Iran's supreme leader Ayatollah Ali Khamenei amid US-Israel strikes. (Banaras KHAN / AFP via Getty Images)

"We have Operation Iron Gate that just went into effect. That means nothing comes in by land," Trump said Sunday.

But the roughly 20-person task force established at U.S. Central Command headquarters in Tampa is taking a quieter approach than Trump’s sweeping description might suggest.

"This is not a deployable special operation task force," Farzin Nadimi, a senior fellow at the Washington Institute for Near East Policy, told Fox News Digital, describing Iron Gate instead as a "fusion cell" coordinating military, civilian and intelligence efforts.

US ECONOMIC CHOKEHOLD ON IRAN REACHES PEAK LEVERAGE AS COLLAPSE RISKS EMERGE

Nadimi said its immediate role is likely to center on gathering intelligence about activity along Iran’s borders and building cooperation in the region that could support sanctions and other efforts to disrupt the flow of goods.

Secretary of State Marco Rubio, who heads the department responsible for spearheading the administration's diplomatic foreign agenda, supports taking action that could decrease military escalation.

"We don't need to conduct military strikes if we're achieving the same objective through economic means and economic pressure," Rubio told reporters on Tuesday.

He rejected the suggestion that the U.S. and Iran were at a stalemate, arguing that sanctions, the naval blockade and other measures were steadily weakening Tehran.

"They’re bleeding. They’re bleeding economically," Rubio said. "It is an increased pressure on Iran that continues to grow every day."

The push comes a week after an election in which the war proved a political liability for Republicans.

The Iran war was a crucial factor in the House vote for 34% of voters, according to the Fox News Voter Poll, while about six in 10 said the U.S. had made the wrong decision to take military action against Iran.

An Iranian oil tanker is seen floating on the Caspian Sea, as Iranian President Mohammad Khatami attends the opening ceremony of the Neka oil terminal on the Caspian Sea, 340 kms northwest of Tehran in Mazandaran province. (HENGHAMEH FAHIMI/AFP via Getty Images)

A separate Fox News/NORC survey conducted around Election Day found 68% said avoiding a prolonged military conflict in Iran was very important. When forced to choose, 61% said ending U.S. military involvement to lower gas prices was more important than restarting military action to stop Iran from developing nuclear weapons, while 35% chose restarting military action.

Yet four days after the election, CENTCOM announced Iron Gate, extending the administration’s pressure campaign to the land routes Tehran could use to blunt the effects of the naval blockade.

CENTCOM said the task force would coordinate efforts to disrupt advanced weapons, dual-use technologies, sanctioned materials and illicit revenue moving across Iran’s land borders and the Caspian Sea.

"We will not allow the Iranian regime to rebuild its weapons factories or replenish its arsenal with materials smuggled across its borders," CENTCOM commander Adm. Brad Cooper said in announcing the task force.

CENTCOM declined to provide additional details about how Iron Gate would operate beyond its announcement.

The challenge is the sheer scale of the territory involved.

Iran shares land borders with Afghanistan, Armenia, Azerbaijan, Iraq, Pakistan, Turkey and Turkmenistan, spanning roughly 6,000 kilometers, according to Nadimi. While it has dozens of official border crossings, goods can also be smuggled through mountainous and desert terrain.

"It will be extremely difficult to know where and how things get in or out of Iran," Nadimi said.

Much of Iron Gate’s effectiveness will also depend on governments outside Iran.

Nadimi said the task force could build relationships with military and intelligence services in neighboring countries to identify and disrupt suspicious shipments. Turkey and Pakistan have already placed some limits on goods moving across their borders with Iran, he said, contributing to long truck lines and higher shipping costs.

But even if neighboring governments cooperate, Iran and the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps are likely to look for ways around the restrictions.

Behnam Ben Taleblu, senior director of the Iran program at the Foundation for Defense of Democracies, said the effort should not be judged by whether Washington can completely eliminate Iran’s overland trade.

"That is not going to mean zeroing out this trade overnight," Taleblu told Fox News Digital. Instead, he said the goal is to increase the cost of moving goods, pressure the foreign companies and individuals facilitating Iranian trade and give Washington more leverage over Tehran.

"This is literally akin to boxing in the Islamic Republic," he said.

The routes have taken on added importance as Iran’s other avenues to the outside world have narrowed.

Taleblu said Iran’s missile program remains dependent on foreign inputs including fuel precursors and high-strength steel. China and Russia are important to those flows, he said, but Iraq, Turkey and Pakistan also represent important regional routes.

The U.S. military has been enforcing a naval blockade in the Strait of Hormuz, even during the ceasefire. (U.S. Central Command)

Nadimi described effectively restricting Iran’s land trade as a "mid- to long-term project" and predicted Tehran would initially "become more aggressive" as the pressure mounts.

"It is entirely plausible that you squeeze them hard enough that they feel like the only way to throw off the strangulation is to throw a punch," he said.

That leaves the administration trying to increase its leverage over Iran without reigniting the full-scale fighting voters strongly signaled they wanted to avoid — while Trump has publicly described the objective in much starker terms.

#5955. And What About Europe’s Center-Left?

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: And What About Europe’s Center-Left?

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

In a few months, Europe will mark a date of great moment. The Treaty of Rome, signed in March 1957, established what came to be the European Union.

The 70th anniversary celebrations will, however, be relatively low-key. Italian Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni has ensured that the biannual meeting of the European Council take place in the Italian capital. There will be tributes, official speeches, but—at the start of another likely turbulent year for the continent—few governments seem interested in hailing an organization that has come to be denounced by some as elitist and out of touch.

In a few months, Europe will mark a date of great moment. The Treaty of Rome, signed in March 1957, established what came to be the European Union.

The 70th anniversary celebrations will, however, be relatively low-key. Italian Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni has ensured that the biannual meeting of the European Council take place in the Italian capital. There will be tributes, official speeches, but—at the start of another likely turbulent year for the continent—few governments seem interested in hailing an organization that has come to be denounced by some as elitist and out of touch.

In immediate political terms, their reluctance might be understandable. The EU has struggled to understand, let alone address, the roots of populism; it has struggled to get its voice heard in the big global conflagrations, from Israel-Palestine to Iran to the Trump administration’s curious love-in with Russian President Vladimir Putin. And it has struggled to find a language that communicates with voters, particularly younger ones.

But symbolic moments such as this provide opportunities for a refresh, just as the results of the U.S. midterm elections appear to point to a liberal world that is belatedly finding a way of standing up to the far right.

Yet, amid all the talk of a “blue tsunami” and the jubilation at the Democrats’ stunning seizure of both houses of Congress, quiet heads are counseling that it is far too early to write off the nationalist extremists who have held politics around the world in a vice-like grip for so long. It is possible that Donald Trump might temper his behavior—but then again, he might not. He might instead use his remaining two years in office to lash out. His mind does not work in rational or predictable ways. And in any case might the MAGA movement find another, more professional, less mercurial standard-bearer?

The issues that did Trump in were the price of gas and the cost of living, not a particular affection for the alternative. Trump’s imperial behavior and the corruption swilling around him and his entourage were contributory factors, but bread-and-butter issues were at the fore. Those same concerns are the center point of the various elections taking place in 2027 in Europe, which threaten to bring down a series of mainstream governments.

Yet underlying them is the more amorphous question of values. The center-left parties, as in the United States and as in Europe, have always felt more comfortable defining themselves against their opponents than knowing what they stand for.

EU membership was supposed to provide not just economic and other practical benefits but also a sense of abiding principle. It became an important part of a wider international architecture that set the parameters for the postwar era. Yet eight decades is a long time, and, Trump or no Trump, no system is guaranteed to, or entitled to, last indefinitely.

Most of the last few years in Brussels have been taken up with firefighting, dealing with Trump’s vagaries, Putin’s military aggression and hybrid warfare, and the trade superpower that is China. Beyond sloganizing, there has been little time or seemingly desire to ponder the wider questions.

Some groups are trying, though, and I took part in one such gathering on the eve of the U.S. midterms. Organized by Friedrich-Ebert-Stiftung (FES), the think tank of Germany’s Social Democratic Party, a couple of dozen politicians, activists, thinkers, and others were brought together at a closed-door retreat near Frascati, a picturesque town southeast of Rome.

We were invited to use four principles as our starting point: democracy, freedom, peace, and social justice. What quickly became clear was the extent to which the first two had been co-opted by the far right with considerable success.

On social justice, the consensus was that despite welfare, pensions, and other state support such as the minimum wage (largely but not exclusively the preserve of the center-left), all sides were struggling badly to address the causes of stagnant growth and either flatlining or deteriorating living standards for many that stretch back to the 2007-08 financial crash, if not longer. Broadly speaking, the center-left is stuck in a spiral of benefits spending, which because of aging and other demographic shifts, continues to increase at a time when debt repayments from borrowing has in several countries reached record highs. Parties on the right and far-right are simply confused. Those in Germany, France, Poland and elsewhere promise continued generous support for the working-class (where possible, excluding migrants); others, such as in Britain and Austria, tend more to embrace small-state, neo-liberalism of the Reagan-Thatcher era.

As for peace, that depended on one’s interpretation. Much of the left finds it hard to reconcile the need to defend Ukraine and demonstrate hard power in the face of wider Russian aggression with an in-built aversion to “militarization.” Spending priorities are frequently expressed by parties such as Germany’s Die Linke (The Left) as a zero-sum transaction: Would you rather your money go to schools and hospitals or military hardware (and pernicious private sector profits)?

It is, of course, a false choice, and the work has only begun to make the public aware of the many dangers posed. The only consolation is that the far right appears at least as confused about how to deal with both Putin and Trump, with many party leaders—from France’s current favorite for president, Marine Le Pen, and Alice Weidel of the Alternative for Germany (AfD) two of many that have hurriedly sought to dissociate themselves from past support they have received from the Kremlin and the White House.

The term “democracy” has been recast by the right as the domain of a “silent majority” that is finally being heard, after being “shut out” during the dominance of the “deep state” of the past. And when they don’t get their way, and voters rebuff them, they cry foul, declaring that election results are the result of manipulation or an in-built bias in mainstream media. This tried-and-true trick beloved of Trump (at least until the recent midterms) was most recently adopted by Flavio Bolsonaro, who was defeated in last month’s Brazilian presidential runoff. Mainstream politicians a different dilemma. What happens when the democratic process—notably elections—puts into power political forces hell-bent on damaging or destroying democratic checks and balances?

The term “Freedom” is similarly fraught. As some delegates at the conference suggested, centrist politicians have found that territory hard to occupy. The most contested space in freedom of expression. Around 15 years ago, I ran a U.K.-based nonprofit called Index on Censorship; in that era, the battle lines were rigidly drawn between dictators (who stopped people from printing or saying what they wanted) and democrats (who ensured that at home while battling for the rights of others abroad). Then came the populists, then came “woke,” and the battle shifted back home and became toxic.

Yet the first signs are emerging that the argument is being reframed and will be healthier for that. Freedom “from”—the “nanny state,” the “deep state,” and the assorted far-right demons—is being clawed back. I heard Europeans talking with increased confidence the language of “freedom to breathe clean air,” as a means of justifying the green policies, and “freedom to be healthy,” to explain health spending.

This is just the foothills. There is much more work to be done. FES and its partners will be issuing a manifesto ahead of next March’s anniversary.

However, during the discussions, the point I continued to raise was that it comes down more to behavior than grand statements. Mainstream leaders need to start demonstrating greater resilience, imagination, and courage. Might U.S. Democrats, long derided as divided and weak, use their victory to show the way? Might Europe, which has been stuck in a doom loop of excessive caution and incrementalism, follow? That will be a major undertaking.

Some see the solution in greater European federalism, the language of “European patriotism.” Enrico Letta, who lasted barely a year as Italy’s prime minister in 2013-14, published a report in 2024 titled “Much More Than a Market.” This provides some useful indicators. But the cause of European liberal democracy is about more than the fate of the EU.

The original Treaty of Rome was a political and moral response to the European catastrophe of World War II that had finally come to end. Might the final throes of MAGA provided a fillip for a reinvention of those principles?

John Kampfner is the author of Braver New World: The Countries Daring to Do Things Others Won’t.
