#5943. Jim Jordan becomes new House GOP leader after closed-door vote, defeating Steve Scalise

Fox News co-host Tomi Lahren reacts to breaking news that Rep. Jim Jordan, R-Ohio, was voted to become the new House minority leader on 'America Reports.'

House Judiciary Committee Chairman Jim Jordan, R-Ohio, will be President Donald Trump's top defender in Congress next year after winning a closed-door election for minority leader on Tuesday, sources told Fox News Digital.

Jordan defeated Majority Leader Steve Scalise, R-La., currently the House of Representatives' No. 2 leader, in what's believed to be a narrow race. Jordan received 106 votes to Scalise’s 73, sources in the room told Fox News Digital.

Fox News Digital heard loud, sustained applause from outside in the hallway when Jordan’s win became official.

House Majority Whip Tom Emmer, R-Minn., also handily defeated Republican Study Committee Chairman August Pfluger, R-Texas, for the No. 2 leadership job of minority whip. The final vote count was 103 to 76, per sources.

Sources said House GOP Conference Chairwoman Lisa McClain, R-Mich., meanwhile, also survived a challenge and kept her job.

JORDAN EMERGES AS POTENTIAL NEXT GOP LEADER IF DEMS SEIZE HOUSE

Rep. Jim Jordan leaves a House Republican Conference meeting at the U.S. Capitol in Washington, D.C., on Dec. 10, 2024. (Tom Williams/CQ-Roll Call, Inc. via Getty Images)

Republicans huddled for a candidate forum on Tuesday morning where all GOP lawmakers running for leadership positions were peppered with questions by their colleagues.

Sources inside the room for their Tuesday morning leadership forum said Scalise and Jordan both pitched themselves as the best person to unify the conference, but also signaled they would give a wide berth for lawmakers to "vote their districts," as one person put it.

The question-and-answer session for House Republicans before electing their leader was largely cordial, Fox News Digital was told. Several people inquired about how both prospective leaders would unify the conference after their midterm losses and how they would handle leaks to journalists, Fox News Digital was told.

Jordan himself told Fox News Digital he believed the forum went well but declined to go further, stating he does not divulge details of closed-door meetings.

The voting body consisted of incumbent House Republicans and GOP members-elect who are projected to win their races from last week's midterms. Votes were cast via secret ballot, meaning none of those in the room had to reveal who they chose.

AMERICA SAW THE BLUE WAVE COMING — BUT DRASTICALLY UNDERESTIMATED THE EXTENT OF THE DEM TSUNAMI

Majority Leader Steve Scalise heads to the House chambers ahead of a vote on a continuing resolution to fund the government in Washington, D.C., on Nov. 12, 2025. (Nathan Posner/Anadolu via Getty Images)

And the results on Tuesday show the House GOP was hungry for change. Jordan's victory comes fresh off the back of a historic loss for Republicans across both state and federal races.

The magnitude of the losses forced Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., to announce he was stepping aside and not seeking another leadership position for the next two years.

"I personally like Steve, but he's been in leadership since before I was in Congress. And Jim Jordan, while he's a leader in Congress, he hasn't been part of the leadership team. He basically rose through the ranks because he was super effective," Rep. Warren Davidson, R-Ohio, told Fox News Digital.

Rep. Scott Fitzgerald, R-Wis., another Jordan ally, told Fox News Digital the Ohio Republican "understands what we're up against," adding, "His ability to communicate is really kind of unrivaled by anybody else in the House right now."

Rep. Steve Scalise, R-La., and Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., attend a news conference at the U.S. Capitol in Washington, D.C., on June 30, 2026. (Graeme Sloan/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

Jordan has long been among the House GOP's brightest stars, rapidly gaining influence over the last decade as one of Trump's most vocal supporters in Washington.

He will now be the go-to defender as Democrats ready a barrage of investigations targeting Trump's Cabinet, family and business interests.

#5942. ‘Sabotage’: Read Hegseth’s Fiery Speech Against His Impeachment

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Transcript: ‘Sabotage’: Read Hegseth’s Fiery Speech Against His Impeachment

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth held a rare press conference on Tuesday morning as he faced the prospect of impeachment, delivering an uncompromising and combative speech in defense of his record while excoriating his political opponents.

“What one disgruntled and defeated congressman is attempting is not oversight, and it’s not accountability. It’s a political spectacle, a personal manifesto driven by ego and resentment. Time for one man’s final days in Congress to try to do damage to this president; this country; and most irresponsibly, the warriors who defend it. It’s a farce, and it’s a stunt, and everybody knows it,” Hegseth said at the beginning of his remarks.

U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth held a rare press conference on Tuesday morning as he faced the prospect of impeachment, delivering an uncompromising and combative speech in defense of his record while excoriating his political opponents.

“What one disgruntled and defeated congressman is attempting is not oversight, and it’s not accountability. It’s a political spectacle, a personal manifesto driven by ego and resentment. Time for one man’s final days in Congress to try to do damage to this president; this country; and most irresponsibly, the warriors who defend it. It’s a farce, and it’s a stunt, and everybody knows it,” Hegseth said at the beginning of his remarks.

The impeachment effort, led by outgoing Republican Rep. Thomas Massie, largely focuses on the Defense Department chief’s central role in the Iran war but also pertains to other major military actions under his watch—including strikes on alleged drug trafficking boats near Latin America and the operation that led to the capture of Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro.

Massie, who introduced eight articles of impeachment against the defense secretary on Monday, accuses Hegseth of waging war against Iran without congressional approval, abusing the powers of his office, and violating federal law and the Constitution.

The House is scheduled to hold an initial procedural vote on impeachment on Tuesday evening.

Below is the full transcript of Hegseth’s speech.—John Haltiwanger

Well, good morning. I’ll get right to it. What one disgruntled and defeated congressman is attempting is not oversight, and it’s not accountability. It’s a political spectacle, a personal manifesto driven by ego and resentment. Time for one man’s final days in Congress to try to do damage to this president; this country; and most irresponsibly, the warriors who defend it. It’s a farce, and it’s a stunt, and everybody knows it.

In the 250 years of our American republic, no secretary of war or secretary of defense has ever been impeached for carrying out lawful military operations, and in our case, successful ones at that. Not once, war powers resolution or not. Not under [former U.S. President Ronald] Reagan in Lebanon, not under [former U.S. President Bill] Clinton in Kosovo, not under [former U.S. President Barack] Obama in Iraq and Syria, not under [former U.S. President Joe] Biden in Iraq and Syria. That’s the standard. The history is clear, and it’s very straightforward. And one disgruntled congressman wants to shred it over policy disagreements. That’s what it is: policy disagreements, not high crimes, not misdemeanors, not treason, not bribery, not the kinds of offenses the founders intended when they wrote the impeachment clause.

The proper remedy for a policy disagreement—it’s legislation, it’s debate, it’s the next presidential election. It’s not handing the House and Senate floor to the Democrats so that they can run a show trial against the president and the secretary of war while Republicans still control Congress and should be delivering wins for the American people. You see, we’ve seen this show before. They impeached [U.S.] President [Donald] Trump over a perfect phone call. They impeached him again in his final week in office. Both times they failed. Both times he came back stronger, and the American people sent him back to the White House.

Now, they’re trying a third time. This time against a member of his cabinet. They will fail again, and if standing with President Trump puts me in that company, I’ll take that company every day of the week.

Let’s be clear about what this resolution actually does. It tells our enemies—Iran, the cartels, every adversary watching—that the United States will actually punish its own warriors and its own civilian leadership for successfully executing lawful military operations. It tells them that a single rogue legislator, loaded with personal animus for the president, can attempt to veto national security decisions made by the duly elected commander in chief. It puts a target on the backs of American warriors by signaling that Congress will undermine their missions in real time. This is not patriotism. This is sabotage.

You know, five years ago, America was humiliated in Kabul, Afghanistan, and every enemy on earth took notice. But somehow, as the president pointed out yesterday—no impeachment vote for [former U.S. Defense Secretary] Lloyd Austin for that terrible withdrawal. Not even one vote. Or no impeachment vote for when he went AWOL for over a week. No impeachment vote for when he let a Chinese spy balloon fly over the country for over a week. See, President Trump ended that kind of wokeness and weakness. Peace through strength, not as a slogan, but as a way of war.

The Houthis found out first. They fired on American ships. Biden had let it happen. President Trump said, “no more.” Operation Rough Rider hit them more than 1,000 times. They cried uncle, and they stopped shooting at our ships to this day. Iran found out next. Operation Midnight Hammer, B-2s from the heartland, halfway around the world, straight into Fordow, Natanz, and Esfahan—flawless. The largest and most successful B-2 strike in American history. Nuclear sites obliterated.

Later in Operation Epic Fury, by every measure, we achieved a historic military victory. Under 40 days of sustained combat, more than 13,000 targets struck. Their navy sunk, their air force grounded, their defense industrial base gone, more than 80 percent of their nuclear industrial base gone, their military in ruin, the ironclad blockade unbroken. We control the Strait of Hormuz. Crazy Islamists in Tehran will never, ever have a nuclear weapon. A historic effort, only made possible by a courageous commander in chief. History will judge the Iran war quite differently than conventional wisdom right now. We didn’t start this war; Iran did, 47 years ago.

But President Trump is willing to do what no other president would. He did the tough, right thing—not for politics, but for the safety and the security of the American people. That’s leadership.

He did not kick the can; he kicked their teeth in. For generations—my generation fought other Middle Eastern wars that dragged on for years, for decades. We lost thousands, thousands of Americans. I wear the bracelet of one of them on my left wrist every single day. It reminds me.

And may I remind you, for perspective, we lost 60,000 Americans in Vietnam over 20 years. We lost 37,000 in Korea in just three years. That many humans are lost on the front lines of Ukraine and Russia every month. This—Epic Fury—has never been that, and it never will be. The media can just never admit the military success because they, you, hate the president more than you love our country or our troops.

Then, in our own hemisphere, we started at home from day one. President Trump made homeland security national security. We sent American troops to lock down the southwest border and choked off the cartel pipeline of human smuggling, of drugs, and death. And then, we took the fight to them. The cartels thought they could just poison the American people and hide from justice. So we formed a counter-cartel coalition, designated them terrorists, and hunted them at sea, and now on land. More than 70 narco-vessels sunk; 218 narcoterrorists dead, where they should be; maritime smuggling down at least 65 percent and dropping even further. Thousands of American lives saved with every drug boat sunk and every shipment prevented.

Indicted and wanted, Nicolás Maduro thought he was untouchable. He was not kidnapped. He was brought to justice by America’s best. Victory in one night. Victory in 48 minutes, to be precise. And today, he sits in front of an American judge in New York City. Flawless success.

Panama kicked China’s Belt and Road Initiative to the curb, and we, along with them, control the canal. The message is simple: This is our hemisphere. Act accordingly.

Our NATO allies, after decades of riding on the American taxpayer in Europe, committed to 5 percent of GDP for their own defense. Our allies are finally stepping up. Out with being a burden, in with burden sharing. Why? Because President Trump demanded it. America is the only country on Earth, and this military the only one, that could successfully execute and lead all of these operations and efforts. Sheer success, victories in both Venezuela and Iran, energy security in our hemisphere, in a way none thought possible for a generation, and no nuclear bomb for Iran ever.

But of course, security doesn’t come free. Nobody was lost in the mission in Venezuela, but 18 Americans have given their lives in Operation Epic Fury. We know their names. I’ve been to Dover. We honor them every day, but their sacrifice only stiffens our resolve.

As it happens, tomorrow is Veterans Day. This year, the operators of Absolute Resolve and the warriors of Epic Fury will mark it for the first time. They stand shoulder to shoulder with the pilots of Midnight Hammer and the sailors of Rough Rider who went before them. They answered the call, they executed, they won, and on the eve of Veterans Day, these resolutions tell every one of them that their service was a crime.

Their service was not a crime. These are heroes, and no politician in Washington will tell them otherwise while I hold this office. President Trump gave this department what it has not had in a generation: a commander in chief with the courage to lead and the resolve to win. I stand behind every one of these operations, I was proud to execute them, and I would do it again tomorrow.

And while this defeated politician—defeated ironically by a Navy SEAL, I might add—while this defeated politician drafts articles of impeachment, we’ve delivered the first increase, and a big one, in hostile fire and imminent danger pay in more than 20 years. We’ve reinstated over 600 service members who were forced out under the previous administration’s COVID[-19] mandate. All five of our services broke historic recruiting records: the Army, the Navy, the Air Force, the Space Force, the Marines for two years in a row. In fact, the Marines in FY27 met half of their goal on day one. Americans are lining up to serve in President Trump’s Department of War. And by the way, to those Marines and all those Marines out there, semper fi and happy 251st birthday today.

Two years of historic recruitment after Biden couldn’t recruit anyone. Same with retention, by the way, through the roof. That means the troops that are in are staying in. The best of the best, based on merit, staying in. Night and day from Biden to Trump. We cut unnecessary senior officer billets by 20 percent and raised combat performance standards. Through the One Big Beautiful Bill, we’re investing in troop pay, in housing, in healthcare, in advanced weapons and equipment our warfighters actually need to win. That’s the record. That’s what we’re defending.

Make no mistake about what is happening. This is not about me. Politicians in Washington have accused the sailors and the pilots who struck narcoterrorist boats of war crimes for doing their job. Senators in cute videos have suggested the same. This resolution is the next step. Put the secretary on trial today, and the men and women who carried out those orders tomorrow. If they want to come for the warriors, they’re going to have to come through me.

As for the articles themselves, Iran, the cartels, Maduro, Yemen. Every one was a lawful operation ordered by the commander in chief, then ordered by myself, notified to Congress, and executed by American warriors who did their jobs. We destroyed terror regimes’ capabilities. We sank the boats carrying poison into our communities. We put a narco-dictator in handcuffs. These are not high crimes. This is the job, and it’s the job well done.

For the first time in American history, a cabinet secretary overseeing America’s military is being attacked not for losing conflicts with our enemies, but for winning them. Condemned not for defeat, but for military victory. The disgruntled congressman even admitted this himself yesterday. No failure here, just impeachment for success.

As to the claim that the American military targets civilians, well, that is a lie and that is a slander against every professional who flew, sailed, and fought on these missions. The only people who target civilians are the terrorists that we hunt, and I will dignify this claim no further. None of these are high crimes or misdemeanors; they’re partisan policy disagreements from people who want to politicize our military.

We will not apologize for putting American lives first. We will not apologize for stopping the flow of drugs that kill our citizens. We will not apologize for destroying the capabilities of a regime that chants “Death to America,” and if that regime makes one wrong move, as the president said, we remain locked and loaded with the full weight and lethality of Central Command. We will not apologize for restoring a military that recruits based on merit, restores real standards, and fights to win.

These resolutions are a farce. They mock the Constitution. They attack the chain of command, and they criminalize the warfighters who execute lawful orders. This process hands our adversaries a propaganda victory and wastes the time of Congress that should instead be focused on protecting the American people.

We will continue to do the job the American people elected President Trump to do: protect this country, defeat our enemies, and rebuild a military worthy of the warriors who wear the uniform.

I pray each day, and I hope you do too, for our troops. They are the best of our nation. Like the president, I will defend them with every fiber of my being, every single day. That includes having their back, no matter what. So to the troops, as I’ve said before: Don’t blink, ignore the noise, never hesitate. This is all politics. You do real things. Stay the course. May Almighty God protect them always, and may our actions today and every day bring glory to Him. We will stand, we will fight, and we will defend this republic against all enemies, foreign and domestic. And amen.

#5940. The True Meaning of Power

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

What the Godfather of Realism Can Teach Trump

“International politics, like all politics, is a struggle for power.” Hans Morgenthau wrote this sentence from his desk in Chicago at the dawn of the Cold War, as the United States and the Soviet Union embarked on an open-ended competition for global dominance. Politics Among Nations, the volume that Morgenthau ultimately produced, would quickly become the most widely adopted international relations textbook in the United States and provide an intellectual framework—realism—that would guide generations of scholars and policymakers.

Although much has changed in the nearly eight decades since Morgenthau’s most influential work was published, the fundamentals remain the same. Human nature, which for Morgenthau drove the struggle for power, is unmoved. States are still the most significant actors in the international system and compete for influence. Great powers continue to drive the course of history. Much of today’s world would seem to affirm Morgenthau’s conclusions: Russia’s attempts to establish a sphere of influence in Eastern Europe, China’s quest for hegemony in East Asia, and the United States’ revival of the Monroe Doctrine in the Western Hemisphere all suggest the enduring primacy of national interests over moral aspirations and international norms.

For many, Morgenthau’s contributions begin and end with his identification of power as the key determinant of state behavior. But this reading misses one of his core observations: that even if humans tend to be power-maximizing creatures, they are also moral beings who seek to limit their own power. Morgenthau’s writings reveal a dialectic between power and morality—two seemingly incompatible concepts that he spent his life attempting to reconcile. Far from romanticizing power, Morgenthau saw the need to tame it.

An immigrant who escaped Nazi Germany and owed his life to the United States, Morgenthau had specific guidance to offer his fellow Americans. Even as he urged the United States to protect its vital interests by maintaining a favorable balance of power in key regions, he implored the country to wield its power prudently. Morgenthau saw the United States as the only country with both the capacity and the will to fight on behalf of transcendent values that he believed power had to serve. He was committed to preserving American power as a check on the violence and bigotry he experienced. Yet he feared that U.S. overreach would hollow out the foundations of that power. This, for Morgenthau, would be a tragedy for humanity.

Perhaps no government has misread Morgenthau more than the second Trump administration. Its officials revel in the exercise of military force around the world, from Iran to Venezuela and the Caribbean, while dismissing concerns of alleged war crimes and threatening long-standing allies. White House Deputy Chief of Staff Stephen Miller typified the Trumpian outlook when, in January, he invoked the “iron laws of the world,” one “governed by strength,” “governed by force,” and “governed by power.”

Some analysts have also erroneously rooted the Trump administration’s actions in realism. A lengthy essay in The New York Times Magazine, for instance, ran under the headline “The Theory That Gives Trump a Blank Check for Aggression.” This is understandable, as the Trump administration has repeatedly claimed its actions are underpinned by “flexible realism.”

But any reading of realism that does not consider the moral dimensions of Morgenthau’s theory is both mistaken and dangerous. When the United States limits its own power, abiding by international norms, consulting allies and partners, and wielding its strength in pursuit of transcendent values, countries find American influence more palatable, which helps sustain it. But if Washington’s actions become untethered from these restraints, others will no longer defer to American prerogatives, and the world will become a more dangerous place.

Returning to the foundations of realism—to the man who introduced this intellectual tradition to Americans—is critical. If Morgenthau were to witness this moment in U.S. history, he would conclude that the country had overlearned his lessons about the importance of power and erred in equating it with the use of force. He would see a need to elevate moral questions and rebuild normative constraints on the exercise of power. And he would warn against foreign entanglements that undermine American democracy, which remains the wellspring of the country’s power. These are the lessons that will help the United States navigate an era of intensified great-power competition, one in which the margin for error is thin and the cost of miscalculation could be catastrophic.

Morgenthau was born into an assimilated, middle-class Jewish family in southern Germany in the winter of 1904. His conservative and authoritarian father, Ludwig, raised his only child to revere the monarchy and take pride in their country’s achievements. Morgenthau’s childhood might have passed as that of a shy, precocious boy in a self-assured bourgeois community, but Germany’s defeat in World War I and the rise of anti-Semitism set his life on a drastically different course.

In 1922, as a top student in his high school, Morgenthau was chosen to address his classmates and neighbors in a public ceremony honoring the school’s namesake. When he began to speak, he noticed that some had turned their backs and others were holding their noses at this “stinking Jew.” Morgenthau finished his remarks, but as he left the square, his classmates hurled anti-Semitic insults and spat on him. Just months later, Hitler descended on Morgenthau’s hometown of Coburg with hundreds of storm troopers, who assaulted protesters while the police looked on. Coburg soon gained the ignominious distinction of being the first town to elect the Nazis to power.

Morgenthau left Coburg hoping to become a writer or poet but was forced to pivot to law after hyperinflation ruined his family’s finances. During his apprenticeship under Hugo Sinzheimer, a leading left-wing labor lawyer, he saw conservative judges undermine the Weimar Republic and aid those who sought its destruction. Morgenthau later reflected in his intellectual autobiography that this led him to conclude that “what was decisive was not the merits of different legal interpretations but the distribution of political power.”

As life for German Jews deteriorated, he fled to Geneva to teach law. Switzerland was unfortunately not far enough away; after Hitler took power in Germany, students began boycotting his classes because they did not want to learn German law from a Jew. He then found a teaching position in Madrid but was displaced by the Spanish Civil War and wandered through Europe before arriving in New York in 1937 as a penniless refugee. Having witnessed the rise of fascism up close, Morgenthau was shocked to find that so many Americans believed that human nature was fundamentally good, that peace was the normal condition of humanity, and that history was a story of progress.

In New York and then Kansas City, Morgenthau grew alarmed as the United States watched Nazi Germany rearm, annex Austria, and invade Czechoslovakia. He believed that a failure to understand human nature had inspired Western democracies’ blind faith in international law and the League of Nations, leading them down the path of least resistance with appeasement of Hitler, straight into the moral abyss.

Shaken by the fall of France in 1940, Morgenthau began writing his first English-language book, taking aim at Americans’ confidence that the methods of the natural sciences could make sense of the social world and that the sheer force of reason would eventually eliminate conflict. In Scientific Man vs. Power Politics, published in 1946, he charged that rationalism and liberalism had misunderstood the nature of man, the nature of the world, and the nature of reason itself. Humans, in addition to having rational needs, possessed irrational impulses that could not be reasoned away. Man’s “lust for power would be satisfied only … if he became like God.” Countries, like humans, sought to maximize their power over one another, making conflict inevitable.

Scientific Man received disastrous reviews, and Morgenthau later recalled that it “shocked people to no end.” He adjusted his approach, tailoring his message to Americans and packaging it in a textbook that would appeal to their pragmatism. He wanted this book to mark an evolution in American thinking about foreign policy, privately writing that it would differ from its predecessors “as an automobile differs from a horse and buggy.” Two years later, in Politics Among Nations, he achieved that goal. Morgenthau stressed that the struggle for power was inescapable. All states sought to keep, increase, or demonstrate their strength. Disarmament, collective security, and international law would not contain this struggle. Only a balance of power could prevent countries from attempting to overthrow the existing order. Power could be tamed only by countervailing power.

It is easy to see why many misread Morgenthau as a champion of realpolitik—an heir to the likes of Machiavelli or Bismarck. After all, he placed power at the center of his analysis. But he never romanticized it. As he wrote in a private letter, “I am an analyst of the nation state and of power and have emphasized time and again their negative moral connotations.” Morgenthau’s personal encounters with power had taught him that its unbridled pursuit led to calamity. He understood that normative and moral limitations were required to keep the struggle for power within tolerable bounds. As he wrote in another letter, “It is the task of civilized societies to temper the power drives and direct them into socially useful channels.”

Indeed, Morgenthau had less tolerance for those who failed to reconcile power with morality than for those who underestimated power’s importance. Although President Woodrow Wilson’s universalism and idealism made him one of Morgenthau’s favorite targets, he preferred him to a Machiavellian prince: “Wilson’s failure was at least the failure of greatness, of too great a vision. Nobody will say as much for the epigones of Machiavelli.” He similarly criticized the British historian and political theorist E. H. Carr, whose moral relativism had led him to advocate for appeasing Hitler. Carr, he wrote, had “no transcendent point of view from which to survey the political scene and to appraise the phenomenon of power.” While both men are seen as pivotal realist thinkers, Morgenthau witheringly concluded of Carr that “it is a dangerous thing to be a Machiavelli. It is a disastrous thing to be a Machiavelli without virtù”—that is, it is ruinous to practice power politics without skill and judgment.

In Politics Among Nations, Morgenthau took issue with leaders who both glorify the struggle for power and reject any limits on their own use of power. He argued that “philosophies and political systems which have made the lust and the struggle for power their mainstay have proved impotent and self-destructive.” And he drew attention to common moral standards that could sustain order and moderate the struggle for power. He observed that throughout history, humans insisted on subjecting political action to ethical scrutiny. When such restraints held, countries did less than their power enabled them to do; once commonplace practices, such as assassinating a rival country’s leaders or killing a large portion of its civilian population to reduce its strength, became stigmatized. Morgenthau concluded, “What has changed is the influence of civilization which makes what is desirable and feasible ethically reprehensible and, hence, normally impossible of execution.”

In the immediate aftermath of World War II, however, he believed that the limitations on the struggle for power had reached their weakest point since the advent of the modern state system. Hitler had attempted to extinguish a whole people, and both the Axis and the Allied powers had bombed cities indiscriminately. Although Morgenthau believed the Allies were morally justified in taking such actions to defeat a genocidal regime, he nonetheless feared that the struggle for power was on its way to becoming boundless. In the nuclear age, failing to reestablish these moral barriers could mean the end of humanity.

Morgenthau’s hard-nosed reputation has long obscured the moral core of his realism. He was unequivocal that power must be a means to an end—“an attempt to realize moral values through the medium of politics.” He recognized that maximizing power for its own sake was both destructive and pointless, arguing that “a nation must pursue its interests for the sake of a transcendent purpose which gives meaning to the day-to-day operations of its foreign policy.” In his view, ancient Greece and Rome are remembered because their power served a purpose that outlived their empires: Athenian democracy and philosophy, and Roman law and republican government.

As he assimilated and raised a family in Chicago, Morgenthau came to see the United States as the inheritor of this tradition. The country’s contribution to the world and the source of its power lay in its democracy and civic life. A realist approach—one that prudently wielded power abroad—was best suited to protect the American experiment at home, enabling Americans to live in freedom and security while offering an example for other countries to emulate. In other words, American power had to be bound to purpose—a purpose the country fulfilled “as a light to attract strangers, not as a flame to be spread throughout the world.” This was not merely the observation of a dispassionate scholar. The United States had accepted Morgenthau when no other country would, and it was a place where immigrants like him could rise to the top of their fields.

By the 1960s, Morgenthau saw that American power faced a threat of its own making. He began to develop deep misgivings about U.S. intervention in Vietnam, warning as early as 1961 that it could become a quagmire reminiscent of France’s war in Algeria. Two years later, as clashes between the South Vietnamese government and protesters intensified, Morgenthau urged the United States to get out. He built his opposition on realist grounds, arguing that a communist takeover of Vietnam would not affect the balance of power in Asia, thus rejecting the domino theory, which held that communism would spread inexorably outward once it gained a regional foothold. Morgenthau also recognized that communism was no longer monolithic and believed that Ho Chi Minh could lead a nonaligned country as Tito did in Yugoslavia. He saw the conflict as a civil war and worried that the United States was attempting to maintain a status quo that was itself the source of instability.

Successive administrations failed to heed Morgenthau’s warnings and instead plunged the United States deeper into the war. He came to resent American policymakers’ misappropriation of his lessons on power and entered public debate with new vigor. At a packed rally in New York’s Madison Square Garden in June 1965, he warned that a guerrilla war could be won only through the indiscriminate killing of the South Vietnamese people, which would tear at the moral fiber of the United States. Later that month, Morgenthau debated President Lyndon Johnson’s national security adviser, McGeorge Bundy, on national television. He led with his moral outrage: “I am opposed to our present policy in Vietnam on moral, military, political, and general intellectual grounds.”

The greatest risk of the war, Morgenthau discerned, was “the risk to ourselves, to our mission in the world, to our very existence as a distinct nation.” He lamented, “Here is the last best hope of the downtrodden and the enslaved, to which men of good will throughout the world have looked as a shining example, relieving its frustration in blind ideological fury and aimless destructiveness upon a helpless people.” Vietnam, in his telling, had corrupted the very purpose that gave American power its meaning.

The Vietnam War validated Morgenthau’s belief that power untethered from questions of morality would lead to overreach. It proved that the United States had an interest in constraining itself, if only to avoid disaster. More fundamentally, Vietnam showed that misadventures abroad could boomerang, weakening American democracy and societal cohesion and, ultimately, undermining the country’s international standing.

The second Trump administration’s foreign policy record has thrown these insights into sharp relief. Its officials view questions of morality as a costly sideshow, the remit of woolly-minded idealists who do not understand how the world works. The White House has time and again resorted to military force, striking boats in the Caribbean, kidnapping Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro, blockading Cuba, and waging war on Iran. It has also threatened military action against Canada and Denmark, two of the country’s long-standing allies.

American democracy, far from serving as “a light to attract strangers,” is fast becoming a cautionary tale. The second Trump administration has undermined the foundations of American power through attacks on the courts, the press, and universities. It has gleefully bulldozed through any checks on its writ. Its assault on institutions and its aggressive foreign policy are intertwined; the administration not only failed to seek congressional authorization for the war in Iran, but it also refused to even make the case to the American people before striking.

President Donald Trump has justified his aggression on the basis of the “iron laws that have always determined global power.” Morgenthau would not have recognized this as realism. He contended that “superior power gives no right, either moral or legal, to do with that power all that it is physically capable of doing.” The Trump administration, by contrast, seems to believe that to not use the power at its disposal is to waste it. Morgenthau’s verdict on Carr applies equally to Trump: he is a “Machiavelli without virtù,” playing power politics without judgment and leading his country to disaster.

Morgenthau would argue that the Trump administration misunderstands the very concept of power, which should not be equated with military might and must instead be understood as a complex psychological relationship. Real power, Morgenthau insisted, lies in the ability to influence a country through consent, not coercion. In this view, the use of force signifies the abdication of political power rather than its culmination. The Trump administration’s belligerence betrays its weakness: its acts of aggression around the world are confessions of its inability to shape events through other means.

Although Morgenthau grew up on the statecraft of Bismarck, he came to admire Abraham Lincoln above all others, even attempting to write a book about him toward the end of his life. Morgenthau saw in Lincoln a rare leader who was never possessed by power, instead possessing it and, ultimately, subordinating it to transcendent values. Today, just as at the height of the Vietnam War, Morgenthau would see his task as restoring morality and ethics to their rightful place and highlighting the need for power to move the world toward justice.

The United States has lost its purpose, and unless it rediscovers it, discrete foreign policy successes will be irrelevant. As Morgenthau wrote, if the country loses its soul, any victories are merely “footnotes to the history of the decline and fall of a once great nation.”

DAVID SACKS is Senior Fellow for Asia Studies at the Council on Foreign Relations and the author of the forthcoming book The Realist: Hans Morgenthau and the Purpose of American Power.

#5939. Peltola widens narrow Alaska Senate lead over Sullivan as counting continues

The Alaska Senate race remains undecided with Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan and Democrat Mary Peltola are separated by fewer than 700 votes. Finn and Dunleavy discuss the impact of same-name candidate Dan J. Sullivan, who received thousands of votes, as the closely watched race remains unresolved.

Alaska's still too-close-to-call Senate race is expected to deliver another batch of ballot results Tuesday night after Monday's latest ballot drop expanded Democratic nominee Mary Peltola's lead over incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan to 2,517 first-choice votes.

Still current as of Tuesday midday ET, Peltola stands at 48.6% (106,321 votes) to Sullivan’s 47.5% (103,804) in the ranked-choice voting system that will kick in if neither candidate gets across the 50% threshold.

The controversial race, punctuated by the "Decoy Dan" scheme exposed by The New York Times, has Republican Gerald Heikes at 2.2% (4,865) and independent Daniel J. Sullivan Jr. at 1.7% (3,651 votes). Roughly 75–76% of the expected vote is in, with tens of thousands of early, absentee and questioned ballots still to be counted.

ALASKA SENATE FIGHT HEATS UP AS SULLIVAN MOVES TO BLOCK PELTOLA LAWSUIT OVER REJECTED ABSENTEE BALLOT RECORDS

"After every vote is counted, it is clear that Mary Peltola will be elected Alaska’s next United States Senator," Peltola campaign manager Cheryl Bruce wrote in a statement Tuesday morning after that previous tranche of votes.

"Mary won today’s votes by a margin of 68-30, reflecting major support in key areas from Anchorage and Chevak. This overwhelming margin further strengthens Mary’s path to victory, and Mary is looking forward to continuing her relentless fight to lower costs and put Alaska first in the United States Senate after all votes are counted."

Monday's drop added nearly 5,000 ballots and marked a shift from the razor-thin, 637-vote Peltola edge that had held for days after Election Day.

The incumbent Sullivan and President Donald Trump have both suggested if Peltola does not get to 50% of the vote and ranked-choice voting (RCV) goes to rounds two or three that the Republican would absorb secondary voting strength in the system.

DOJ FACES ‘HEAVY LIFT’ IN ALASKA ELECTION APPEAL AS SEPARATE CRIMINAL PROBE CONTINUES

Under Alaska’s system, if no candidate reaches a majority of first-choice votes, the lowest finisher is eliminated and ballots transfer according to voters’ rankings until one candidate surpasses 50%. Right now, that would be the other Dan Sullivan, which might help Peltola break 50%.

The Official RCV tabulation is scheduled for Nov. 18 after the overseas absentee deadline.

The slow pace matches the timeline Gov. Mike Dunleavy described in an interview Sunday. He called the state’s process "very confusing" and "a slow system," pointing to significant ballot dumps expected Friday, Nov. 13, and Wednesday, Nov. 18.

Dunleavy has also continued an investigation into the "Decoy Dan" candidacy of Daniel J. Sullivan Jr., which some Republicans allege was intended to siphon votes from the incumbent; the governor has said the Division of Elections and the Department of Justice are reviewing related questions.

TRUMP ADMIN PUTS ALLEGED 'DECOY DAN' CONSPIRATORS ON NOTICE IN ALASKA ELECTION SPOILER PROBE

Sen. Dan Sullivan, R-Alaska, is facing a larger gap behind Democratic Senate nominee Mary Peltola in the most recent ballot drop Monday night. (Andrew Harnik/Getty Images; Nathaniel Wilder/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

"We're still in the process of bringing in votes and counting votes," Dunleavy said Sunday. "And we are in the midst of investigations as to determine if there was any wrongdoing, especially anything that might resemble perjury or, you know, a misrepresentation of individual's intent on how they wanted their candidacy to be placed on the ballot."

Other statewide races remain fluid on the same ballot count:

MURKOWSKI SNUBS TRUMP-BACKED ALASKA CANDIDATE, ENDORSES DEMOCRAT FOR GOVERNOR

Democrats have won control of the Senate for the final two years of President Donald Trump's final term, holding a 54-44 edge with both Alaska and the Mississippi Senate races still too close to call. Democrat Scott Colom leads incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith, R-Miss., by about 2,500 votes (49.08%-48.81 %).

If Democrats win both those seats, their party would hold a 56-44 edge in the upper chamber, having gone 9-0 in seats flipped in these midterms.

#5937. There’s Never a New Middle East

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: There’s Never a New Middle East

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

This year’s op-eds and magazine features induce déjà vu. The 2003 invasion of Iraq was supposed to clear the way for a new, democratic Middle East. The 2006 Lebanon War was, in U.S. Secretary of State Condoleezza Rice’s phrase, the “birth pangs” of a new Middle East. The Arab Spring in 2009 was its dawn. Hamas’s assault on Israel in 2023 was said to be ushering in a new Middle East.

The U.S. attacks on Iran and the fight over the Strait of Hormuz are said to be bringing yet another one into being. Per U.S. President Donald Trump, “The Middle East will be transformed, and the future of that region has never—I don’t think it’s ever looked brighter.” Foreign Affairs weighed in with an article titled “The Iran Imperative: How America and Israel Can Shape a New Middle East.”

This year’s op-eds and magazine features induce déjà vu. The 2003 invasion of Iraq was supposed to clear the way for a new, democratic Middle East. The 2006 Lebanon War was, in U.S. Secretary of State Condoleezza Rice’s phrase, the “birth pangs” of a new Middle East. The Arab Spring in 2009 was its dawn. Hamas’s assault on Israel in 2023 was said to be ushering in a new Middle East.

The U.S. attacks on Iran and the fight over the Strait of Hormuz are said to be bringing yet another one into being. Per U.S. President Donald Trump, “The Middle East will be transformed, and the future of that region has never—I don’t think it’s ever looked brighter.” Foreign Affairs weighed in with an article titled “The Iran Imperative: How America and Israel Can Shape a New Middle East.”

Bloody and chaotic events like the Iran war induce fantasies of comprehensive change, but reality keeps coming up short. As Sam Rayburn, House speaker in an earlier era observed: “Any jackass can kick down a barn, but it takes a carpenter to build one.” Decisive change for the better—in the United States’ conception—demands an actual preponderance of U.S. military power and the ability to convert that power into enduring political arrangements. The United States has imagined itself as wielding both attributes while possessing neither.

The use of force halfway around the world requires political leaders to show that the stakes are commensurate with the cost and risk. That means war aims are framed in extravagant terms that are eventually revealed as detached from quotidian reality.

On the other side, adversaries have agency. They are not passive objects of U.S. power projection or a substrate onto which Washington can project its fantasies. And, ironically, the weakness and fragmentation of regional states makes it more, not less, difficult for an outside power to impose its will.

When the war ends, as it eventually will, Iran will still be a poor, militarized oil exporter with two good friends—China and Russia—and a strong relationship with Iraq. In short, not terribly different than it was before the war.

Iraq will look much as it does now, a state with a hybrid army trying to fend off pressure from Iran, with which it shares a long border, and from the United States, which controls its finances.

Syria will look as it did the day before the United States and Israel struck Iran. It will be poor, shattered by 13 years of civil war and many more of sanctions, run by a sectarian clique, penetrated by outside powers from Turkey to Israel and Saudi Arabia, and still riven by ethnic and religious rivalries.

Lebanon will still be living with the results of the 1989 Taif Accord, which ended Lebanon’s civil war by rebalancing power among its sects and calling for every militia to disarm. Under Syrian tutelage, every militia did except Hezbollah, which kept its guns as “the resistance.” This, in turn, inadvertently but predictably locked in Hezbollah’s status as an armed rival to the state and a picker of pointless fights with its far more powerful neighbor to the south. It will still be engaged in the Sisyphean task of satisfying the International Monetary Fund (IMF) without a parliament willing to decide who absorbs the banking system’s losses.

Egypt, whose economy suffered badly when the Houthis were firing on ships in the Red Sea two years ago, will still be the same authoritarian state, propped up by Gulf largesse and embarked on gigantic infrastructure projects, that it was the day before the war.

Jordan, necessarily careful given the mayhem on its Israeli, Syrian, and Iraqi borders, will look exactly as it did before the war. So, too, will Libya, Morocco, Algeria, and Sudan—which will all be what they were last year and the year before that.

True, there is the odd beneficiary. The largely unrecognized country of Somaliland has collected strategic rent from the UAE, Israel, and the United States as a geographical counterweight to Houthi domination of the Bab el-Mandeb. But one breakaway enclave doth not a new Middle East make.

What about the Arab side of the Gulf? Every state there has been pummeled by Iranian missiles and drones. Every Gulf state but Oman will see its economy shrink this year. The IMF projects contractions of 16.7 percent for Kuwait, 15.6 percent for Qatar, 11.3 percent for Bahrain, and smaller ones for the UAE and Saudi Arabia. The IMF expects a sharp rebound next year, but only on the generous assumption that traffic through the strait returns to normal during 2027. Crude flows are already at prewar levels even if the process of moving it is cumbersome.

Most of these states are talking about huge new pipeline or transportation projects, whether from Abqaiq to Yanbu, Habshan to Fujairah, or Basra to Baniyas, to make their export routes redundant and therefore more resilient to Iranian or Houthi attacks. Plans for such projects are nothing new for them, and most will probably never be built. All will rush to rebuild their own air defenses now that the United States has shown itself to be relatively ineffectual as a wager of wars but still an eager arms dealer, source of intelligence, and defense industrial partner. This won’t have a significant effect on their militaries, which, except for the UAE, do not have an expeditionary function, but it will improve their resilience in the face of future Iranian and Houthi attacks.

How does this war end? One way is for the Trump administration to sign a concessionary cease-fire that restores normal traffic through the strait and frees the U.S. Navy to redeploy. A Democratic U.S. Congress could hasten that choice. It would not stop the war outright, since Trump would veto a war powers resolution, and Democrats would likely lack the votes to override him.

A fight over funding would still make the war harder to sustain but hand Trump a win-win situation. Instead of going down as the president who lost the war he started, he could blame the loss on Democrats. The Gulf states would then fall back on their traditional, prudent, and strategy of hedging. They would strike side deals with Iran to fully open the strait while holding onto as much U.S. intelligence, technology, weaponry, and expertise as they can, even if the Fifth Fleet were no longer headquartered in Bahrain.

This would look a lot like the over-the-horizon posture—whereby the United States keeps its forces just outside the region, mostly at sea and at a few access points, and relies on prepositioned equipment and basing rights to surge in a crisis instead of stationing large forces on land. This stance served a strategy of offshore balancing, which kept the United States out of a region’s day to day, intervening only when a single power threatened to dominate a region that it considers vital.

The other way is that the war does not end, at least not soon. This seems to be the outcome that U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent and U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth prefer, and on its own terms, it is working. Bessent’s satellite imagery shows no tankers at Kharg Island, and Gulf crude, moving under U.S. escort, is leaving the region at or above prewar volumes.

Why settle? Because Iran gets a vote. A strategy of economic attrition gives Tehran every reason to show that the attrition will cost Washington more than it is willing to pay. That is the purpose of the Houthi strikes on Saudi Arabia, the attacks at Dhahran and Dammam, and the threats against U.S. bases. Since the midterm elections, the president has been signaling a disinclination to escalate pending the outcome of talks with Iran, while insisting that the blockade will continue. This in turn will leave the door open for Iranian attacks on shipping that could prompt the escalation Trump is seeking to avoid. A third carrier group is on its way, ensuring that he will have the combat power he needs to follow through.

If the war does drag on, something will change, though not in the way that the prophets of a new Middle East intend. When the Theodore Roosevelt arrives in the Arabian Sea, three quarters of the Navy’s deployed carriers will be allocated to the war. With the arrival of the Roosevelt in the Andaman Sea, there is now no U.S. carrier in the Western Pacific. A region that absorbs this much U.S. power is changing the United States’ position in Asia. The Middle East is remaking U.S. strategy, rather than the other way around.

The only other major player is Israel. For years, observers have noted the rightward drift of Israeli politics, the collapse of left-wing parties, a more militant posture toward the region, tense relations with the United States, hardening opposition to a two-state solution, and the development of a long-range strike capability meant to disable Iran’s nuclear and missile programs and, as it turned out, to kill many of its leaders. This trend was likely to end in war sooner or later; but it was Hamas’s late leader, Yahya Sinwar, who pushed the button.

The result has been catastrophic for Gazans and for Palestinians more generally. It drove Israeli forces across the borders of Syria and Lebanon and created the widespread impression that Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu lured Trump into the war. These events have accelerated the souring of American opinion about Israel and the U.S.-Israeli relationship in ways that were anticipated a decade ago—particularly the narrowing of political support for the relationship and its transformation into partisan football—well before the current Gulf War began.

This latest pointless war has demonstrated both the persistent American desire for a new Middle East and the inability to make it happen. The 2016 and 2024 U.S. elections were, in part, a referendum on U.S. interventions in the region. Trump appeared to understand that and made it part of his pitch: “I’m not going to start a war,” he declared in 2024, “I’m going to stop wars.”

But restraint has never really been his lodestar, as long as others were footing the bill and he could posture as a warlord. That’s too bad for dead Iranian schoolgirls, U.S. soldiers, and families back home struggling with war-induced inflation.

As for a new Middle East, let alone a better one, the experiment to run is not to test yet again whether military intervention leaves the region better off, since the answer to that is known, but whether less intervention does so.

That experiment is likely to be run by the next U.S. administration, which will answer to a public that sees the Iran war as a mistake, a gratuitous return to the military adventurism that voters repudiated in 2008. Colossal debt and demand for spending on the lives of ordinary people will squeeze the defense budget. Congress, for better or worse, will be looking to the security of the Western Pacific and Europe first. The Middle East will have survived a great deal of American help. It may yet survive less of it.

Steven Simon is a distinguished fellow and visiting professor at Dartmouth College and the author of Grand Delusion: The Rise and Fall of American Ambition in the Middle East. He spent 20 years in the U.S. government, holding senior positions in the State Department and at the National Security Council. X: @sns_1239

#5936. Democrats Shouldn’t Squander Their Chance to Launch a Foreign Policy Revolution

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

MATTHEW DUSS is Executive Vice President of the Center for International Policy. From 2017 to 2022, he served as Chief Foreign Policy Adviser to Senator Bernie Sanders.

The Imperative of Ambition in a New Congress

Tuesday’s election made clear that endless wars, political corruption, and the high cost of living are three of the American public’s top concerns. They are deeply interrelated—and the new Congress must address all three. That will require a true foreign policy revolution that grapples not just with the outrages and failures of the Trump administration’s foreign policy but also with the ways in which past Democratic or Democrat-supported foreign policies have contributed to today’s crisis. There will be a temptation among some Democratic leaders, in Congress and beyond, to limit the scope or pace of change. But to find a way out of today’s crisis and move beyond the failures that have afflicted American foreign policy for the past several decades, Democrats will need to have the courage to face a hard truth: that President Donald Trump’s abuses of power and unilateral warmaking are not so much a break from Washington’s militarist mindset as they are a particularly aggressive, predatory expression of it.

The incoming Democratic majority will face a national security environment in which the most severe challenges to U.S. safety and prosperity come from within. That’s why Congress should shut down the Iran war immediately but also recognize that it can make the United States more secure only if it pairs a foreign policy shift with a frontal assault on spiraling costs and political corruption. Political polarization; government corruption and dysfunction; increasing dominance of political and economic life by a small handful of mercurial oligarchs; and a president who recklessly and unilaterally launches unauthorized military interventions while relentlessly seeking opportunities to enrich himself, his family, and his cronies—all pose more immediate danger to the safety and prosperity of the American people than does any foreign enemy.

When Democrats take control of Congress in January, they will have considerable power to challenge the corruption and malfeasance of the Trump administration. Control of committees and of the congressional calendar will provide the opportunity for sorely needed oversight. But although the Trump administration deserves aggressive investigation, Democrats must aim higher—and use the opportunity to articulate a genuinely compelling alternative for American foreign policy.

The first and most urgent task will be ending the Iran war, a reckless and unpopular conflict that has become even less popular as Americans have felt its effects on their wallets. Congress must respond to widespread bipartisan anger among voters at Trump’s war and abuse of war powers by using all the tools it has, especially its power to cut funding. It has been encouraging to see Democrats continuing to oppose any supplemental budget request by the administration for the war. It is an area where Democrats have gotten tripped up in the past when they’ve been afraid of accusations that they don’t “support the troops” if they oppose funding for foolish, counterproductive wars. With Democrats having held firm against any additional funding for the war, those days appear to be gone. That is good.

But Democrats need to go further. They should be prepared to oppose all funding for the military, including blocking votes on the National Defense Authorization Act, the annual bill setting out the broad scope and budget for the Defense Department, in order to put an end to hostilities in Iran. In fact, not only should they be willing to withhold funding from the Pentagon, but they should also be willing to withhold funding for the entire government, and threaten another government shutdown if the bill does not prohibit any funding for Trump’s war. December 11 is the deadline for a continuing resolution to keep the government funded and operating. If ever there was a reason for Democrats to threaten to withhold their votes, stopping a blatantly illegal and globally destructive war that American voters have now resoundingly rebuked is it.

Democrats must also be willing to take a hard look at the continuity between Trump’s use of military violence and past uses of force by administrations of both parties, and the bipartisan support for that violence. The groundwork for the Iran war was laid by the Biden administration’s unstinting support for Israel’s genocide in Gaza (as even some former administration officials have acknowledged), but also by years of U.S. refusal to impose any consequences on the Israeli government despite clear evidence of ongoing violations of human rights and international law. Trump’s strikes on alleged “narcoterrorist” drug boats in the Caribbean represent the logical evolution of long-standing treatment of the United States’ drug problem as a “war,” and of casually applying the label of “terrorist” to justify any and all measures, including execution without due process. Seeking accountability for these abuses will require acknowledging the past complicity of both Republicans and Democrats.

Democrats must also take on corruption, a key node connecting the country’s domestic and foreign policy crises. In doing so, they will be walking through an open door. Polls consistently show that political corruption is high on Americans’ list of concerns. According to a recent Harris poll conducted for The Guardian, “Nearly nine out of 10 Americans said that government corruption, as defined as elected officials putting their own or their donors’ financial interests ahead of the public’s, is a problem.” A mid-October CNBC All-America Economic Survey found that voters are more concerned about government corruption than any other issue, including high grocery and gas prices. In September, Gallup reported that “Americans’ perceptions of government corruption in the U.S. are at their highest level in 20 years, with 89% of U.S. adults saying it is widespread.”

To say that the Trump administration provides a target-rich environment for a strong anticorruption push is to commit felony understatement. Exposing foreign gifts to Trump and conflicts of interest by family members, contracts awarded to Trump’s oligarch donors and new tech buddies, and the selling of pardons, as well as restoring enforcement of the Corporate Transparency Act, the Foreign Corrupt Practices Act, and related task forces, should all be part of the plan. Democrats should drill down into Trump’s wildly corrupt “Board of Peace” for Gaza, which U.S. Representative Jamie Raskin, the Maryland Democrat set to chair the House Judiciary Committee, rightly referred to as a “global slush fund.” The obvious conflicts of interests of Trump’s diplomatic envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner also demand investigation.

The good news is that effective anticorruption does not necessarily require new legislation; it merely requires that existing legislation be enforced. An August 2025 essay in Foreign Affairs, co-authored with my colleagues Casey Michel and Trevor Sutton, laid out a more expansive case for Democrats putting anticorruption at the center of their foreign policy platform. (A recent essay by Casey Michel lays out a more detailed agenda for the new Congress.)

Ultimately, the most important step toward tackling corruption will be reforming the campaign finance system. The United States is an outlier among developed democracies in that its system of campaign finance allows for what is essentially legalized bribery. Legislators speak openly about campaign contributors expecting “returns on investment” for their political donations. This is an outrage. In recent years, foreign actors have increasingly begun bankrolling American legislators, taking advantage of massive loopholes in campaign finance laws and the Federal Election Commission’s lack of enforcement.

Confronting this will be no small task. At a minimum, Congress must move to legislate a response to the Supreme Court’s 2010 Citizens United decision, which effectively greenlighted bottomless campaign funding from American corporations. It must also reform the Federal Election Commission, providing the body with much-needed resources to investigate campaign finance crimes and restoring the practice of allowing congressional appointments to the commission, which has become little more than an appendage of the executive branch.

All of the above steps must be connected to the top concern among American voters at this moment—the high cost of living. Democrats have disagreements in several policy areas, particularly in foreign policy. Their coalition is diverse. But there is consensus around addressing Americans’ material needs urgently and aggressively. Such an approach would confront Trump’s reckless use of tariffs (while recognizing that the neoliberal taboo on tariffs is a thing of the past), rising gas prices because of the Iran war, the need to restore the global minimum tax, the threat of job losses to AI, and the increased power of tech oligarchs over Americans’ economic and political lives, including on foreign policy, as Elon Musk’s restriction of Ukraine’s Starlink connection demonstrated. The approach should involve a mix of tactics, including hearings, oversight investigations, and legislative proposals.

Many of these efforts may not produce new laws in the next two years. But they could become the backbone of a winning agenda for Congress and for a Democratic administration in 2029. Most important, they would represent a step toward the forging of a new American political consensus on which a new and durable foreign policy can be built.

#5934. Shanghai-based tycoon Neville Roy Singham's 'Squad,' Bernie Sanders donations resurface

Fox News Digital's Asra Nomani spoke with Jodie Evans during a New York City protest that took place while Israeli Prime Minister Bibi Netanyahu delivered remarks at the United Nations General Assembly.

A Shanghai-based tycoon known for pushing Chinese Communist Party talking points, whose activist network is flooding Capitol Hill this week, has directly funded some of the most powerful progressives in Congress, including members of "The Squad."

Federal Election Commission records show that Neville Roy Singham and his wife, CodePink co-founder Jodie Evans, have given more than a combined $47,000 to Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., and Reps. Pramila Jayapal, D-Wash., Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., and Chuy García, D-Ill.

Omar's campaign took $4,200 from the couple from 2019 to 2023, according to FEC records. Singham gave her campaign $2,700 in March 2019, just weeks after she took office, and Evans gave $1,500 in two contributions in November and December 2023. None of it has been refunded.

HOUSE PANEL TO PROBE FOREIGN ‘TROJAN HORSES’ AS SINGHAM SCRUTINY WIDENS

Federal Election Commission filings show Singham and his wife, Jodie Evans, directly contributed to a number of Democratic members of Congress. (Jim Vondruska/Getty Images; Dave Kotinsky/Getty Images; Valerie Plesch/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

Sanders' campaigns have taken in at least $6,800 from the couple, according to FEC records, most of it for his presidential bids. Singham gave $2,700 to Sanders' 2016 presidential campaign. Evans gave the 2016 campaign $2,800, of which it refunded $200, and at least $252 to his 2020 campaign in 2019. She also gave $1,250 to his Senate campaign committee, Friends of Bernie Sanders, in 2011.

García's campaign took in $1,000 from Singham in August 2018, according to FEC records. He was running for his first term in Congress at the time, after winning the Democratic primary for Illinois' 4th Congressional District, Singham's home city of Chicago. The campaign has not refunded the contribution.

Jayapal has received more from the couple than any other member of Congress, a net $35,700 from 2016 to 2022, according to FEC records. Singham gave her campaign $17,800 and none of it was refunded. Evans gave $22,900, and the campaign refunded $5,000 of it in three payments between 2020 and 2023, leaving $17,900. Most of the couple's money came in 2019 and 2021, including back-to-back maximum contributions from Singham in both years.

Rep. Ilhan Omar eats lunch in Karmel Mall of Somalia in Minneapolis. Minn.. Friday, October 23, 2026. (Rashid Umar Abbasi for Fox News Digital)

Under Federal Election Commission rules, a campaign has 10 days to decide whether to deposit a check or return it.

Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez's, D-NY, campaign returned a $500 contribution from Evans through ActBlue on the day it arrived, Aug. 4, 2018, during her first run for Congress.

Fox News Digital reached out to the offices of Sanders, Jayapal, García and Ocasio-Cortez and Omar's office for comment but did not receive responses.

DHS, WAR DEPT JOIN PROBE INTO SINGHAM NETWORK ALLEGEDLY SOWING DISCORD IN US

After his wedding to Evans in 2017 and the sale of ThoughtWorks, the technology company he founded, Singham directed $285 million to nonprofits and media organizations, according to a Fox News Digital investigation. The People’s Forum received $22.4 million from Singham, while CodePink received $1.33 million. BreakThrough BT Media Inc., which operates BreakThrough News, received $1.098 million, according to tax filings. Singham and his wife, who are both American citizens, now live in Shanghai, China.

Organizations within the Singham network regularly participate in staged anti-ICE, anti-capitalist, anti-data centers and anti-Israel protests – many of which garner national and social media coverage.

FROM AUSTIN TO NEW YORK, PRO-CHINA NETWORK FUELS WEEK OF ANTI-US PROTESTS AS XI MEETS TRUMP

Last month, Evans and CodePink co-founder Medea Benjamin embarked on a China propaganda tour across the communist country.

The trip, according to an event promotion link for sign-up on the CodePink website, was 12 days and 11 nights in Shanghai, Chongqing, Zunyi, Loushanguan, Maotai Village and Guiyang with the cost of attendance between $3,000 and $4,000 depending on the total number of participants. The CodePink website said attendees will be "greeted by local Shanghai comrades," and a tour with Benjamin and Code Pink co-founder Jodie Evans is included.

Jodie Evans and Neville Roy Singham are pictured together. (Getty Images)

"Explore China’s revolutionary history, while marveling at high-tech infrastructure and modern socialist development," the event description reads. "Highlights include Red Memorials, Historical Re-Enactments and Museums, Socialist Infrastructure, and Rural Revitalization."

Singham’s wife’s California-based nonprofit does not shy away from their mission to rebrand China as an ally – displaying the tagline "China is not our enemy" on their website while actively promoting the "revolutionary history" of the communist country.

HOUSE PANEL SUBPOENAS 3 LEFT-WING GROUPS IN PROBE OF $39M TIED TO MARXIST TYCOON NEVILLE SINGHAM

A slew of federal and congressional investigations have been opened into the Singham network, including a grand jury probe led by the Department of Justice and the Southern District of New York – though Singham has yet to be charged with any crime.

Neville Roy Singham speaks at the Global South Academic Forum in Shanghai, China, in November 2025, in a conference blessed by the Chinese Communist Party. ((L) Global South Academic Forum, (R) Getty Images)

During a House Foreign Affairs Subcommittee on South and Central Asia committee hearing in September, Mike Gonzalez, a senior fellow at the Heritage Foundation, cited research saying Singham shares premises with Maku Cultural Communication, a Chinese communications firm that promotes a favorable image of China, and was photographed attending a Chinese Communist Party strategic communications workshop in 2023.

JIM BANKS, GOP LAWMAKERS RALLY BEHIND DOJ PROBE INTO ALLEGED CCP-LINKED FUNDING NETWORK

In a video that Fox News Digital unearthed, Singham stood at the Golden Tulip Hotel in Shanghai during a November 2025 conference and expressed support for "a new world order that is based on multilateralism that President Xi and the CPC [Communist Party of China] and China have proposed."

Earlier this year, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent traveled to New York City for a meeting with Goldman Sachs Chairman and CEO David Solomon, and sources told Fox News Digital that Bessent gave a blunt ultimatum to Solomon.

In November 2017, at the Global South Academic Forum in Shanghai, Neville Roy Singham (2nd from right) expresses support for the Chinese Communist Party and calls the West "dangerous," as Marxist academic Vijay Prashad (right) listens. (Global South Academic Forum)

Bessent told Solomon that Goldman Sachs could face scrutiny for alleged conspiracy in the funneling of the Singham money and urged Solomon to cooperate with federal investigators.

A person familiar with the meeting told Fox News Digital that the discussion wasn't contentious and that Solomon readily agreed to pledge his cooperation with the Justice Department investigation. A spokesperson for the bank said "all distributions from Mr. Singham’s donor-advised fund were made to legal nonprofits, as determined by the IRS. There have been no distributions from the account since August 2023, and it was closed in early 2024."

Fox News Digital reached out to Singham, CodePink and Evans but did not receive responses.
