#5869. Supreme Court seems split over federal agencies' ability to fine for law violations

The U.S. Capitol is seen behind signage for U.S. Department of Labor in Washington, D.C., in August 2025. Jim Watson/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

At the Supreme Court, the justices seemed divided on Tuesday in a case that could undermine the ability of many federal regulatory agencies to fine businesses that violate federal laws.

For decades, farmers have relied on a federal immigration program that allows them to temporarily employ migrant workers to pick crops.

In addition to wages, farm owners must provide basic necessities, such as housing, access to meals and water. The whole system is essentially policed by the Department of Labor to ensure that foreign workers don't undercut domestic laborers.

In that role, the department fined Sun Valley Orchards, based in New Jersey, a half-million dollars for unsanitary conditions, including providing unsafe drinking water, housing people in 20-bunk rooms, broken sinks and other violations, including failure to pay promised wages.

Sun Valley is disputing the charges and contends that the Department of Labor imposing monetary fines based on its administrative proceedings is unconstitutional. Sun Valley also says the process denies them the right to a jury trial when and if they appeal to a federal court.

At the high court Tuesday, Chief Justice John Roberts strongly suggested that the Trump administration, and many administrations before it, have exceeded the power of the executive branch. In this case, potentially wading into the judicial branch's territory.

Addressing Deputy Solicitor General Sarah Harris, who represented the Labor Department, Roberts said, "It seems to me you're just trying to leverage … the broad authority [of the executive branch] beyond the justification for its application."

Justice Sonia Sotomayor asked how disruptive it would be for the federal courts to enforce the law instead of the designated agency (in this case the Department of Labor).

Harris responded succinctly, "If the court were to say civil penalties always have to be in Article III courts, that's kind of the nuclear option."

Harris added that such a ruling would disrupt programs as diverse as Medicare, Medicaid, the federal student loan program and more.

That prompted this question from Justice Elena Kagan: "Why not just go to court always?" Harris quickly responded, "I think it would be catastrophic." She went on to explain that a system like that would inevitably result in massive under-enforcement of the law, given limited resources.

Lawyer Robert Johnson represented Sun Valley Orchards. "To be sure," he said, "the statute contemplates penalties, but it says nothing about where those penalties should be adjudicated."

Johnson further contended that penalties should be imposed by the federal courts, not the administrative agencies that do most of that work under current law.

To that Justice Ketanji Brown Jackson replied, "It seems like you're just sort of fighting the reality of … what Congress was trying to do … in ensuring that we don't have farm workers coming in and undercutting domestic workers."

A more skeptical observation came from Justice Brett Kavanaugh: "What you're proposing is really at war with clarity," he said, adding, "I think that's not great systematically going forward."

Justice Samuel Alito pointedly added, "What you're outlining is sort of a hodge-podge opinion, and maybe sometimes courts write that sort of thing," to which Justice Kagan quickly chimed in with a chuckling crowd in the court, "Not ever us!"

In rebuttal, Deputy Solicitor General Harris was unusually candid. "It would be a big deal for the government to lose, but an even bigger deal would be to have a mess," she said. She later added, "A lot of other statutory regimes also would be on the cutting-room floor."

And if that's the case, she said, the administration will have to go to Congress. Her voice rising, she added, "Please tell us now ... if we need to get Legislative Affairs on it right now, because this will snowball."

#5857. House votes to effectively end the impeachment effort against Pete Hegseth

Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth takes questions from reporters during a news conference at the Pentagon on Nov. 10, 2026. Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images hide caption

The House has voted 211 to 204 to refer the articles of impeachment filed against Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth to committee, effectively killing the impeachment effort spearheaded by Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky.

Tuesday's vote short-circuits any potential floor vote on the articles of impeachment, and virtually guarantees the measure will not be taken up before the current session of Congress expires.

The vote provided a surprise reprieve for President Trump's embattled Defense Secretary, who was facing an impeachment vote by the full House as early as Wednesday.

Massie reintroduced the articles of impeachment on Monday, accusing Hegseth of high crimes and misdemeanors in connection to the war with Iran and U.S. strikes against Iran and on alleged drug boats in the Caribbean Sea and Eastern Pacific Ocean.

Massie's impeachment resolution sought to oust Hegseth over the capture of Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro without congressional authorization, and the effort to punish Sen. Mark Kelly, D-Ariz., for a video telling service members they can refuse illegal orders.

Hegseth defended himself against the impeachment effort during a rare press conference at the Pentagon on Tuesday, criticizing Massie as a "disgruntled and defeated congressman."

"I stand behind every one of these operations," Hegseth said. "I was proud to execute them, and I would do it again tomorrow."

On Monday, Trump expressed support for Hegseth during a wide-ranging, impromptu press briefing at the White House.

"I'll stand by him," Trump said. "He won two wars. He was in charge. We all won."

The impeachment measure was Massie's second attempt against Hegseth. He first sought to bring the impeachment resolution to the floor before the midterm elections, prompting Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., to call an early recess to avoid the vote. That caused the resolution to expire, which is why Massie reintroduced the resolution again this week.

While the impeachment push may be quelled for now, Hegseth's troubles on Capitol Hill are not over. Democrats have promised vigorous investigations into Hegseth's Pentagon once they assume the majority in January.

Whether they will mount another impeachment effort remains unclear, but in an interview with NPR last week, Rep. Hakeem Jeffries, who is expected to be the next Speaker of the House, said simply of Hegseth, "He's got to go."

#5844. PowerBank completes 3-MW New York community solar project

Renewable energy developer PowerBank has completed the 3.26-MW Camillus community solar project in Camillus, New York, and has received permission to operate. Solar Advocate Development, LLC, the project’s owner and EPC, built the array on a capped landfill, which it acquired from PowerBank in spring 2025 in a $7.3 million transaction.

“A closed landfill is land that most people would write off,” said Andrew van Doorn, president and COO of PowerBank. “At Camillus, our engineers and construction partners turned it into a 3.26-MW source of clean power for local households. Reaching permission to operate on a site like this reflects decades of hands-on experience, and it’s exactly the kind of project where that experience matters most.”

The project will soon accept community solar subscriptions. Solar Simplified will manage customer acquisition and subscription services for this community solar project.

#5835. ARRAY Technologies and Shoals plan to merge companies

Solar tracker and racking manufacturer ARRAY Technologies and electrical balance of systems (eBOS) provider Shoals Technologies have initiated a stock-for-stock merger to combine both companies.

“This transaction represents a compelling opportunity to create meaningful value for customers of both ARRAY and Shoals by bringing together our complementary strengths. Although structural and electrical systems must operate seamlessly together in the field, they have historically been designed and sourced through separate channels. This combination changes that. Together, we expect to deliver a differentiated, technically integrated and interoperable platform that simplifies project execution, enhances project economics and accelerates innovation for customers,” said Kevin Hostetler, CEO of ARRAY.

According to the press announcement, ARRAY will be Shoals’ parent company after the transaction, with Shoal’s CEO Brandon Moss assuming the role as CEO for the overall company. ARRAY’s CEO Kevin Hostetler will transition to a special advisor role for the company.

“Today marks an important milestone for Shoals, ARRAY and the customers we serve. By bringing together two highly complementary businesses with experienced teams, we are creating an integrated platform with broader capabilities, deeper customer relationships and a stronger foundation for long-term growth. We believe the combined company will be positioned to better service customers, deliver more innovative and integrated solutions, realize meaningful synergies and pursue expansion opportunities across the solar value chain, data center markets and beyond,” said Brandon Moss, CEO of Shoals.

The companies plan to create packaged warranties across their structural and eBOS products. As manufacturers, both ARRAY and Shoals have supplied their products to solar and energy storage projects globally.

ARRAY is known for producing single-axis solar trackers that use an inter-row rotating mechanism. Over the years, the company has iterated on its trackers to use multi- and dual-row architectures, and to be deployed on undulating topographies. These rotating solar structures are supported with proprietary tracker operations software with considerations such as shading and hail and wind stowing.

Shoals manufactures combiner boxes and cable management solutions for multi-market scale solar and energy storage projects, as well as data centers.

“Having had the privilege of serving as chairman of both ARRAY and Shoals, I have seen firsthand the complementary products, talented teams and shared commitment to customers offered by both companies,” said Brad Forth, chairman of the board of directors of ARRAY and Shoals. “Together, ARRAY and Shoals are well positioned to deliver enhanced value to customers, shareholders and employees. I also want to thank Kevin for his leadership and important contributions to ARRAY, as well as his valuable insights as he transitions to a Special Advisor role. I look forward to continuing to work with Brandon, who brings proven leadership, deep industry expertise and strategic vision to lead the combined company in this next phase of growth.”

Both ARRAY’s and Shoals’ boards have reportedly unanimously approved the merger, with Forth abstaining from the vote. The merger is expected to complete in Q1 2027.

While not mergers, ARRAY Technologies has been acquiring companies producing technologies complementary to solar trackers. Last year, ARRAY acquired fixed-tilt racking and foundations manufacturer APA Solar Racking, and just acquired Affordable Wire Management, a cable management solutions provider, this past September.

#5828. EDP Renewables’ 250-MW Arkansas solar project slated for 2028 completion

EDP Renewables North America broke ground on Cypress Knee Solar, a 250-MW solar energy project in Chicot County, Arkansas. The project is supported by a long-term power purchase agreement with Facebook parent company Meta.

Cypress Knee Solar is expected to be completed by 2028.

“Breaking ground at Cypress Knee Solar marks an important milestone for the project and for Arkansas’ growing role in America’s energy future,” said Sandhya Ganapathy, CEO of EDP Renewables North America. “As energy demand continues to rise, projects like Cypress Knee Solar help strengthen grid reliability while creating jobs, generating meaningful local revenue and delivering long-term benefits to the communities that host them. We are proud to partner with Meta and local stakeholders to advance new energy infrastructure that supports economic growth and American energy independence.”

At peak construction, the project is reportedly expected to support several hundred jobs. Once operational, Cypress Knee Solar is expected to contribute $25 million in tax revenue to Chicot County, which can help fund schools, infrastructure improvements, emergency services and other community priorities.

EDPR NA also contributing more than $77,000 to organizations and programs in Chicot County, including support for the restoration of the Lakeside High School greenhouse, resources for the Lakeside Robotics team and funding for the Lake Village food pantry.

#5816. Solar carport project at California medical plaza solves tricky energy billing

Watthub Renewables and Sunrock Distributed Generation completed a 322-kW solar photovoltaic system at Irvine Heritage Medical Plaza, the three-story Class A medical office building in Irvine, California. Solar modules were installed to carports covering approximately 108 parking spots, turning bare asphalt into shaded, covered parking and into the building’s primary source of electricity.

Watthub Renewables, Sunrock Distributed Generation and SunRenu Solar completed a 322-kW carport solar project at Irvine Heritage Medical plaza in Irvine, California. Credit: Watthub Renewables

The system is expected to generate approximately 515,690 kWh in its first year, covering the majority of the plaza’s annual electricity consumption. It was delivered under a 25-year power purchase agreement requiring no upfront capital from Irvine Heritage’s ownership. Watthub and Sunrock DG financed, engineered and contracted by SunRenu Solar. Sunrock DG now owns and maintains the array, while the property buys the power it produces at a rate below Southern California Edison’s.

Irvine Heritage Medical Plaza was built in 2008 on just over two acres, with approximately 40,000 sq ft of medical suite.

“Medical office is one of the few property types where the parking lot is part of the patient experience,” said Aaron Moston, director at Watthub Renewables. “Somebody’s coming out of a medical or dental procedure at two in the afternoon in August. Whether their car is 140 degrees or 95 degrees is not a small thing. Carports were the right structure here before we ever ran the production model — the fact that they also happen to be the best south-facing real estate on the property is what made the project work.”

Irvine Heritage Medical Plaza is master metered, meaning the entire building draws electricity through a single Southern California Edison (SCE) account on the GS-3-TOU commercial rate, and that cost flows through to tenants inside their charges rather than through individual utility bills. For years, that meant every practice in the building carried a share of an electric bill it could not see or control in a utiliyt territory with rate increases and, during the summer, on-peak energy.

Since solar generation is netted at the master meter, the savings are not captured by one tenant — they reduce the building’s total electricity cost.

“A master meter is usually described as a disadvantage — tenants have no visibility into their usage and the owner absorbs every swing in the energy bill,” said Wilson Chang, CEO at Sunrock DG. “For a solar project it’s the opposite. One meter, one interconnection, one bill to offset and every suite in the building benefits automatically. It’s the simplest way to bring solar to a multi-tenant property.”

Earlier this year, developed another 300-kW solar carport project for the Foothill Medical Association in Foothill, California. The system was also commissioned with a PPA.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

sure would b nice to know solar effect on bill r there demand charges time if use etc. what r real savings?

Drew, yes, this customer does have demand charges associated with their bill but solar is helping reduce that charge. The real savings for this site is about $70k in the first year.

On this site the savings come from energy (kWh) charges, not demand charges. Every kWh the array makes, whether the building uses it or it’s credited back under NEM 2.0, replaces SCE’s energy charge with Sunrock’s lower PPA rate. Net of the PPA payment, that works out to about $0.03 per kWh avoided from SCE. The array is modeled at 515,690 kWh in year one, and the plaza paid nothing up front. Cutting demand charges meaningfully would take storage, which wasn’t in scope here.

#5808. Electric Buses Need a Plan for the Next Operating Contract

Greater Manchester is entering a new stage of bus franchising. In September 2026, Go-Ahead announced that Go North West had won two Bee Network operating contracts covering 38 routes and approximately 60 buses, with service beginning in March 2027. Eighteen of those routes will operate from a new depot in Leigh. The award is a reminder that operating companies can change while the depots, charging infrastructure and grid connections supporting the passenger network remain in place for decades.

Electric-bus procurement adds a long-lived physical commitment to contracts that are often much shorter. An operating agreement may run for five to seven years. A depot building, substation or multi-megawatt electricity connection can remain useful through several such contracts. Buses can sometimes be transferred between operators, but land, cable routes, electrical capacity and installed charging equipment are tied to particular sites. Those assets need to support the passenger service even when the company responsible for driving the buses changes.

Consider an authority retendering electric-bus routes. The incumbent has used a convenient depot and financed chargers and a substantial grid connection. A competing operator submits an attractive price and a credible operating plan, but has no assured right to use the depot and electricity infrastructure. It might need to negotiate with the incumbent, find alternative premises or secure another grid connection, potentially adding years of work and substantial costs. A nominally competitive tender can become much less contestable once those obligations are included.

South Yorkshire has taken a deliberate approach to this problem. In March 2026, the South Yorkshire Mayoral Combined Authority announced the purchase of five major depots, adding them to the publicly owned Leger Way depot in Doncaster. First Bus and Stagecoach will lease the acquired premises back during the transition. The authority intends to use the estate for franchised operations and electric-bus infrastructure, with its first franchised services scheduled for September 2027. Six operators reached the next procurement stage for the initial packages at Olive Grove and Leger Way.

This does not establish that every city should own every depot. London has long maintained a publicly specified, competitively operated bus network while many garages remain outside direct Transport for London ownership. Secure leases, access rights, assignable contracts and specialist infrastructure providers may preserve the same continuity, depending on the local market. The practical test is whether a qualified successor operator can obtain the depot, buses, charging capacity and operational information required to take over on the contracted date.

Remaining asset value is central to that test. If an operator finances twenty-year charging infrastructure under a five-year contract, it needs to know what happens to the investment if another operator wins the routes. The authority must also avoid paying for a useful electrical connection and then finding it cannot make that capacity available to the next contractor. Depreciation, compensation, asset transfer, warranties, software access and grid-connection permissions need to be settled before the first contract ends, ideally before it starts.

England’s January 2026 bus-franchising guidance recognizes the importance of depots, charging infrastructure, transfer arrangements and competition in subsequent procurements. The engineering of electric buses has advanced considerably. Public authorities now need procurement structures that keep the fleet and its supporting infrastructure useful as routes, contractors and technologies evolve.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing article examines the second-contract test through Greater Manchester and South Yorkshire, including asset access, residual values and operating continuity. It draws on my open report, The Bus Is Not the System, comparing metropolitan bus governance and electrification across Britain and Ireland.

#5804. Faropoint building 1st of 16 New Jersey community solar projects

Faropoint, an industrial real estate manager, has started construction on a 1.6-MW community solar installation on the roof of its building in Fair Lawn, New Jersey. The installation is the first of 16 planned Faropoint community solar projects in the state, which will have a total capacity of more than 10 MW.

Solar contractors install the first solar modules on the first solar project in Faropoint’s 16-project portfolio in New Jersey. Credit: Faropoint

The other 15 projects, totaling approximately 8.5 MW, have been accepted into New Jersey’s Community Solar Energy Program. Nine of them have received a notice to proceed, allowing construction to begin.

“We aim to create positive community impact, increase access to clean energy and become a responsible long-term owner in the markets where we operate,” said Brian Nave, VP of asset management and renewable energy at Faropoint. “As one of the top-five small-bay industrial owners in New Jersey, we believe our scale comes with a responsibility to drive meaningful sustainability initiatives. Utilizing previously unused portions of our buildings allows us to support the communities where we invest.”

The 16 projects are expected to generate approximately 12.7 million kWh of electricity a year, which is about the annual electricity use of more than 2,000 homes.

“Managing solar across a large real estate portfolio takes persistence, coordination, and a lot of work behind the scenes,” said Yaniv Kalish, CEO of SolarKal. “Seeing the first of Faropoint’s New Jersey community solar projects move into construction is a major milestone. And with nine more projects cleared to proceed and a half dozen more behind them, that’s real progress for Faropoint and the communities these projects will serve.”

SolarKal, Faropoint’s independent solar advisor, has advised the firm on dozens of projects across its portfolio, providing financial analysis, competitive procurement, developer evaluation and selection and ongoing project management. SolarKal also helped Faropoint select developers able to use available federal clean energy incentives before they are phased out.

Faropoint and SolarKal are also working on community solar projects in Illinois, New York and Maryland.

#5802. Two New Commissioners Headed to the Arizona Corporation Commission

Phoenix — Come January, the Arizona Corporation Commission (ACC) will be bi-partisan as two Democrats, Clara Pratte and Jonathon Hill, have been elected to the ACC. They will be joining current Republican commissioners Lea Márquez Peterson, Rachel Walden, and René Lopez. The election comes as the Commission is considering rate increases for Arizona Public Service Company (APS) and Tucson Electric Power Company (TEP), and mechanisms that would allow both utilities to increase rates every year with less stakeholder input, and as the Commission begins to consider the utilities’ proposed long-term resource plans.

As a recent Sierra Club report makes clear, the ACC wields a tremendous amount of power over the future of Arizona’s energy landscape, public health, and utility bill increases, especially as the state experiences a fossil gas rush to power data centers. Arizonans experience some of the highest electric bills in the country, with some of the most planned gas in the country and with Arizona’s largest utility, APS, opting to run the dirty Four Corners coal plant for more than another decade. Yet, up until now, the Commission has made a habit of increasing ratepayer bills, opting for expensive fossil fuels and cutting programs that require utilities to utilize solar energy to generate electricity and to conserve energy, lower electric bills, and invest in energy efficiency.

In response, Sierra Club Grand Canyon Chapter Director Sandy Bahr issued the following statement:

“These election results should act as a wakeup call to the rest of the Arizona Corporation Commission. Arizonans are tired of being put on the backburner, bearing the burden for expensive fossil fuels that harm our neighbors and contribute to climate change. We share in the optimism of our fellow voters and will continue to carefully monitor the ACC as our work to promote clean air, clean water, and healthy communities and a healthy climate enters a new chapter.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5798. Hyundai Motor Group Outlines Future Mobility Technology Roadmap at FISITA World Mobility Summit 2026

HWASEONG, Korea — Hyundai Motor Group (the Group) is co-hosting the FISITA World Mobility Summit 2026 with the International Federation of Automotive Engineering Societies (FISITA). The two-day summit is taking place Nov. 4–5 at Rolling Hills Hotel in Hwaseong, Korea.

Founded in 1948, FISITA is a global organization that brings together automotive engineering societies and industry experts to promote knowledge exchange and technological innovation in automotive engineering. It holds the World Mobility Summit every two years, bringing together executives and technology leaders from global mobility companies to discuss industry challenges and future technology directions, such as artificial intelligence (AI) and vehicle electrification.

This year’s summit is the first to be held in Korea. Chang Hwan Kim, Executive Vice President and Head of the Electrification Energy Solutions Tech Unit at Hyundai Motor Group, is the first Korean to serve as FISITA President.

“Hyundai Motor Group will continue to strengthen its competitiveness in key future mobility technologies and continue to participate in technology exchange and collaboration across the global automotive industry to contribute to the development of the future mobility sector.” –Chang Hwan Kim, Executive Vice President and Head of the Electrification Energy Solutions Tech Unit at Hyundai Motor Group

On the first day, Manfred Harrer, President and Head of R&D Division at Hyundai Motor Group, delivered a keynote address introducing the Group’s electrification strategy. It encompasses battery electric vehicles (BEVs), hybrid electric vehicles (HEVs), extended-range electric vehicles (EREVs) and fuel cell electric vehicles (FCEVs). Harrer described the Group’s technology development roadmap for responding flexibly to market changes and customer needs.

Harrer also emphasized strengthening future vehicle development capabilities by combining engineering technology with AI-based digital capabilities and connecting the strengths of different technologies to pursue a sustainable mobility future.

In his opening remarks, Kim said that the automotive industry is undergoing an unprecedented technological transition. He emphasized that diverse technology approaches and stronger global collaboration are important to the advancement of future mobility.

Under the theme “Embracing the Complexities of Mobility,” the summit is addressing key technologies and challenges facing the automotive industry, including software-defined vehicles (SDVs), AI, autonomous driving, batteries and energy infrastructure.

Executives and technology leaders from across the global automotive industry are in attendance, sharing perspectives on key technology challenges and future development directions for the mobility industry.

Keynotes and panel discussions are examining major changes in the automotive industry, including advances in AI and software, the evolution of autonomous driving technology and the diversification of electrification technologies. Participants are also discussing technology directions and ways for the industry to collaborate in response to these shifts.

Major automakers are presenting vehicle and technology exhibits during the summit. The Group is displaying the Genesis GV90 and Hyundai TUCSON, along with a battery pack, hydrogen tank and robotics platform.

#5795. You Need A New Car? Go Electric! It’s Superior Technology! Part 1

Your car is getting “long in tooth,” or maybe your daughter is going away to college and you are giving her your car. Whatever the reason, you are looking for another car. It could be new, it could be used: don’t even look at gas cars, electric cars are superior technology!

The wheels on every railroad locomotive in the world are driven by electric motors. All the busiest rail routes in Europe and China are pure electric, including high-speed rail. 98% of new cars in Norway are electric, over 80% of new cars in Denmark are electric. In China, the country with the largest sales of new cars in the world, the percentage of new electric cars in September 2026 was 67%. 82% of the operating bus fleet in China is electric. Even ships and airplanes are going electric. The data is unequivocal: electric propulsion is superior to internal combustion propulsion. Only the huge political contributions to Trump by the fossil fuel industry and his resulting anti-EV policies have slowed down adoption in this country.

In places we’ve been like California; Portland, Oregon; and northern Utah, you are seeing a lot of Teslas, so you know that electric cars are working for a lot of people these days. It’s time for you to do some research on electric cars.

You do pay a premium for new electric cars, because the batteries are still quite expensive. If price is the driving factor for you, buy a used electric car. Hardly any used EVs are high mileage, and EV batteries still have plenty of life even after they go off warranty at 8 years and 120,000 miles. There are thousands of lightly used EVs sitting at used car lots at reasonable prices all over the country these days. Some used car dealers will give you an estimate of the battery life. If not, get a friend who is an experienced EV driver to help you evaluate the battery life of the EV you are considering.

If you just do local driving or have two cars and have a garage or off-street parking, an EV is a no brainer. EVs are hands-down more convenient than gas cars for local driving. Your EV will have plenty of range for local driving, it will get a complete charge overnight in your home garage refueling station and it will never need an oil change.

EVs are as quiet as mice, as smooth as kittens, and as quick as jackrabbits. They have simple electric motors, not complex internal combustion engines. They have simple one-speed transmissions, not complex 7-speed automatic transmissions. They don’t have exhaust systems with mufflers that will rust and need replacement. They don’t have emission control systems with limited life. They don’t have complex cooling systems with big radiators. You seldom use your brakes with an EV, so your brakes may last the life of the car. They don’t have a big lead-acid battery for starting, with a maximum life of 5 years. New EVs have only a small lithium-ion battery, which lasts 10+ years. Bottom line: Routine maintenance for EVs is almost nil. I think the only real maintenance item on a Tesla over the first 3 years is the cabin air filter. You can expect an EV to go 500,000 miles without major repair or replacement in most cases.

Perhaps you can stomach buying a car from Tesla whose CEO has become one of the most despicable persons on the planet lately. (Example: Musk recently suspended access to his Starlink communications system to the Ukrainians who are valiantly defending their country from the Russian aggressors.) However, Tesla has sold almost 10 million automobiles worldwide that don’t emit CO2 and particulate pollution while operating. Tesla also has by far the most ubiquitous charging system, which allows cross-country driving on major US roads with little planning. Consider buying a Tesla.

So, what about cross-country trips? Everyone knows that long-distance trips in an electric car are inconvenient or even impossible, because there aren’t enough charging stations and it takes an hour if you can find one. Wrong: Every 100 miles along all the major Interstate highways, Tesla has built Superchargers with 8 to 32 and a few with over 100 charging stalls. By the time you have gone to the bathroom, walked the dog, and chatted with a fellow EV owner for a few minutes, you probably have more than enough charge to continue your journey. Usually, charging takes about 20 minutes. If you need a longer charge, you may need to watch a bit of Octopus Teacher or Back to the Future II on the big screen in the car for another 10 minutes.

News flash! Since February 28, 2023, most Tesla Superchargers (now North American Charging Standard/NACS chargers) are usable by all EV brands. Unfortunately, older Tesla Superchargers can’t be used by other brands, so I would still advise buying a Tesla if you want carefree cross-country driving. CCS chargers which are native to most other brands have sprung up by the thousands in the US, which together with the newer Tesla Superchargers make it possible to travel cross-country with non-Teslas on many routes with some planning. Also, Teslas can use non-Tesla chargers. Non-Tesla chargers often come with Tesla cables now, so Tesla owners don’t even need an adapter in most cases. However, even Teslas require careful planning if you are driving far from major highways.

My wife and I have done cross-country trips from Utah to Wisconsin for 7 years, on to North Carolina (once), and back to Utah. We have also traveled from Utah to Southern California at the same 500 miles per day rate that we used to do with our gas cars … and with no worries, mate.

I’m going to try to pass on some things I’ve learned from driving five different electric vehicles over 12 years and some 200,000 miles. See part two, coming soon.

If you find any of my articles helpful to you, please use my referral link when buying a new Tesla: https://ts.la/arthur73734 (Be sure to use it when you make your order). Tesla keeps changing the program, but here’s the latest: If you are buying a new Tesla Model 3 or Y and use my link, you will get two months of free Full Self Driving (a ~$200 value) on top of the one month that comes free with every new Model Y right now. This will get you a total of 3 months free Full Self Driving. It will be $99/month after that. For a Cybertruck, you will get $1000 off your purchase price. At this moment, Tesla is offering a year of free Supercharging with the purchase of a new Model 3 Premium or Performance.

Also: Research Meteorologist (Emeritus) at NASA GSFC, Adjunct Professor at Viterbo University On-Line Studies, PSIA L2 Certified Alpine Ski Instructor at Brighton Utah Ski School.

#5792. The renewable energy industry weighs in on the midterms

Democratic legislators have wrested the majority of both the House and Senate from the Republicans’ grasp following a historic midterm election this week. The clean energy industry is clamoring for a sea change in federal support for renewables after the Trump administration razed incentives and funding since taking office in 2025.

While each candidate’s stance on clean energy isn’t apparent — nor is the effect this midterm election will have on domestic renewable energy markets — Democrats and left-leaning legislators have historically supported renewables more than their counterparts across the political aisle.

Wood Mackenzie is predicting that permitting reform will be a high priority for this new class of federal legislators, because of the rising costs of fuel and electricity from oil supply pressures brought on by the United States’ war with Iran and data center proliferation. However, the firm is also predicting that President Donald Trump could exercise his executive power to hamper proposed federal renewable energy subsidies.

“While we see the likelihood for permitting reform, how policy is legislated and implemented remains a risk to the U.S. economy,” said David Brown, director, global integrated research for Wood Mackenzie. “Entrenched partisanship could fuel policy stagnation and regulatory confusion.”

Representatives from the renewable energy industry and environmental organizations provided statements following the midterm results. Heres’ what they had to say.

Energy affordability was a key issue in this election, reflecting the real pressures facing American households and businesses. The solar and storage industry looks forward to working with newly elected federal and state leaders to bring more affordable, reliable power online and deliver the energy savings Americans are demanding.

– Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association

Families and businesses need affordable, reliable power, and strengthening America’s solar manufacturing base in a bipartisan manner will help deliver lower electricity bills. Over the past several years, American manufacturers have invested billions of dollars in new factories, expanded domestic production and created good-paying jobs in communities all across the country. With electricity demand rising, solar remains one of the fastest and most affordable ways to bring new power onto the grid, and the next Congress has an opportunity to ensure that power is American-made.

That starts with reinstating the domestic content incentives, so factories Congress helped bring online have a strong domestic market for American-made solar. It also means robust enforcement of our trade laws to ensure a level playing field for American solar manufacturers and continued investment in next-generation technologies so we can compete with, and leapfrog, China. We look forward to working with the new Congress to advance these shared priorities, keep electricity affordable for American families and strengthen our energy security and industrial workforce.

– Yogin Kothari, chief Strategy officer of the SEMA Coalition

Today, the clean power industry directly employs more than 400,000 Americans, and the U.S. has nearly 388 GW of clean power online, enough to power more than 83 million homes — but we’ll need a lot more than that to meet skyrocketing demand. Modernizing our outdated permitting process is key to unlocking over $800 billion in new infrastructure and 350-plus GW of new clean energy, keeping prices in check and the lights on at home for families and businesses across the country.

– Jason Grumet, CEO of the American Clean Power Association

Since their inception, competitive power markets have delivered billions in consumer savings while spurring innovation. They are the best way to make sure power generation investments are disciplined and efficient, with companies, not ratepayers, bearing the risk. EPSA looks forward to working with lawmakers on both sides of the aisle on policies that keep the grid reliable, protect customers and bring down costs in the long run.

– Todd Snitchler, president and CEO of the Electric Power Supply Association

The message from voters is clear: Americans want greater economic security, relief from rising costs and an energy system that works for them. This election put a bright spotlight on how this country’s outdated energy system and dependence on fossil fuels leave families and businesses exposed to unaffordable price shocks … Some of the biggest opportunities for progress may come at the state and city level. New governors, state legislatures and mayors have the power to move quickly on solutions that address the needs of their constituents: strengthening local economies, creating jobs and lowering bills. From ensuring data centers provide real benefits to communities to expanding clean energy, modernizing the grid, enabling affordable and accessible housing, and improving public transit, state and local leaders can walk-the-talk that climate action delivers real benefits for people.

– David Widawsky, director of World Resources Institute United States

Regardless of election outcomes, access to affordable, reliable energy and energy infrastructure remains essential to nearly every aspect of our daily lives, from powering our homes and businesses to keeping the cost of everyday goods and services within reach. As these leaders prepare to take office, we encourage them to make energy access, affordability, and reliability a priority.

We are ready to work with policymakers on both sides of the aisle to advance policies that support the development of critical energy infrastructure to create jobs, strengthen our nation’s energy security and ensure families and businesses have access to the energy they need.

– David Holt, president of the Consumer Energy Alliance

We just witnessed the collective power voters showed to beat back the Trump regime and fight for their country. Trump and his GOP cronies tried to drive our economy into the ground, but the American people stood up and made it clear that they reject Trump’s disastrous agenda.

We are committed to working with the incoming Congress to hold the administration accountable for the harm they have done to working people and pass legislation to build a clean economy that works for us all — not just billionaires. Creating opportunity for workers and communities while addressing environmental challenges is not just good policy, it’s also good politics.

– Jason Walsh, executive director of the BlueGreen Alliance

#5791. You Need A New Car? Go Electric! It’s Superior Technology. Part 2

Some reasons why you shouldn’t be worried about buying an electric vehicle.

Everyone knows that electric cars are much more inconvenient than gas cars. After all, there is a gas station on every corner and it only takes 5 minutes to fill up. Electric chargers are rare, and it takes at least an hour to charge.

Wrong: If you have access to 220 volt electricity or a L2 charger at home or work, an electric vehicle is much more convenient than a gas car for 300 miles per day of local driving.

In principal: In the evening, you drive into your garage, carport or driveway and take 10 seconds to plug in, the next morning, you have a full charge and take 10 seconds to unplug.

Compare that with a gas car. You probably have to make a couple of special trips per week to the gas station, put in your credit card, grab the smelly nozzle, put it in your car, and wait five minutes to fill. Then you return the smelly nozzle to the pump.

With the gas car, every 3,000 miles, you need to go to a Jiffy Lube and sit in their smelly waiting room for an hour while they change your oil.

With the gas car, every year, you need to go to an inspection station and wait an hour in their smelly waiting room while they do an emissions check.

These three tasks are all eliminated with an electric vehicle, which makes it much more convenient for local driving.

How do you charge at home? Most electric cars come with an EVSE (Electric Vehicle Service Equipment) cable. If not, you can buy one for ~$200. On one end of the cable is the plug that goes into your car, and on the other end is an interchangeable plug. The one that comes with most cables is a standard 110V plug, but for $40 you can buy a 220V Nema 14-50 plug. Almost all RV parks have NEMA 14–50 sockets. If you have 220V service in your garage, you can have one installed for about $200. Northern Lights Electric installed one in my garage in Three Lakes Wisconsin for $130. An electrician in Saint George, Utah, installed one in our garage there for $250. It cost about $500 to have 220V service run to my garage in Lindon, UT. I had a L2 charger installed there for another $700, which I needed for my first Nissan LEAF in 2014. The L2 charger is not necessary if you have an EVSE, you only need a NEMA 14–50 outlet.

L2 charging gives you about 28 miles of range in one hour. Most EVs these days have more than 300 miles of range — so, in principle, you will charge from dead empty to full in ~11 hours. However, I am rarely at dead empty and I normally only charge to 80%, so usually I am only charging from one to five hours. Note: If you want your battery to last as long as possible, don’t charge over 80% or let the charge go below 20%. This is easier to do if you have a bigger battery.

L1 (110V) charging only gives you about 4 miles of range per hour. In principal, if you plug in next to your shaver in the bathroom, it would take three days for a full charge. However, if you drive fewer than 100 miles/day, this works.  We have friends who would need to upgrade the electrical service to the whole house to get 220V service to the garage, and they have gotten along fine with L1 charging. This works especially well since they have a second car. They also have a clothes dryer near the garage. They are able to use its 220V service or nearby Superchargers in special cases.

What if you live in a condo or other place where you can’t install your own charger? With considerable effort, my brother was able to get 220V service for L2 charging in the garage of his condo. If you have a L2 charger at work, it solves the problem. Also, there are many Superchargers in cities, and if you live near one, you can fill up there just as you would with a gas station.

The lowest price Tesla is the Model 3 RWD with seating for five, a range of 321 miles, at a price of $37,000 (see mine in the lead photo). That is plenty of range for both local and cross-country driving. However, if you want to up the range to 363 miles, you need to get the Model 3 Premium for $47,500. For only $3,000 more, you can get the base Model Y. It costs only $40,000. (See the photos below.) With the Model Y, you get a lot more room for baggage, you ride a little higher, you get a much bigger power liftgate, and you have the option of a factory-installed receiver/tow hitch, which is the one upgrade I chose because I often carry my ebikes on a rack in back. The standard Model Y seats five, but you can get the optional YL which seats 6 in three rows of captain chairs. I upgraded from my 2019 Model 3 to a base 2026 Model Y recently. You can get the premium Model Y with better external and internal styling and 363 miles of range for the RWD Model. It also comes with numerous other features, like faster charging, better audio speakers, and a small video screen for the rear seat passengers for another $5,000, as shown below. Four-wheel drive is a $2,000 option on all models and does reduce your range a little.

Every Tesla comes equipped with smart cruise control. Unfortunately, autosteer was eliminated recently unless you pay for Full Self Driving. If there is a car in front of you, you essentially have 100% self-driving until you need to make a turn. You have full automation in a traffic jam, where the car will move forward, automatically following the car in front of you.

I’m currently driving a base 2026 Model Y with FSD V14, which now costs $99/month. I can get into my car in my garage or a big parking lot or a Supercharger and without touching anything say, Hey Grok, then verbally say: Navigate me to [nearly any business, my home, or any address I dictate to the navigation]. Then FSD will drive me automatically out of my garage or the parking lot to the location requested and park my car in a parking lot or on the street (but not yet into my garage). I sit back and “supervise,” but seldom must intervene or complain about its actions as it drives.

“Tesla has introduced a new feature called Automatic Collision Evasion, which can autonomously activate Full Self-Driving (FSD) to avoid imminent collisions, even if the driver has not engaged the FSD system. This feature aims to enhance safety by allowing the vehicle to take evasive actions during critical situations.”

Note: In my opinion, Tesla customers that don’t opt in for the $99/month FSD subscription are missing a very important, possibly live saving safety feature.

There are literally 161 different new EV models to choose from in the US today. There are also several EV models that have been discontinued that you can buy used. If you can’t stomach buying a Tesla from Elon Musk’s company, you can choose one of those.

I will present two good alternatives to buying a Tesla: The top selling EVs in the US in 2026 to date right now are as follows. You can see that the Teslas are by far the most sold, but the Hyundai IONIQ 5 and the Toyota bz are reasonable alternatives for Musk haters.

Toyota led the movement towards automobiles that are more friendly to the environment with the Prius over 20 years ago. However, they were very slow to offer a compelling pure battery electric vehicle. That has now changed, Toyota’s bz EV has received excellent reviews. I had been a big customer of Toyota gas cars until recently, so I dropped down to the nearest Toyota dealership to see the status of EVs there, as shown in the photos below. I was pleased that the dealer had four bzs in stock and I took one for a quick spin. It had the usual excellent Toyota workmanship, with spectacular acceleration like all EVs. However, the driving automation, like in the Corolla I had rented in the spring, is lame. The lane assist will only work on gentle turns.

If you find any of my articles helpful to you, please use my referral link when buying a new Tesla: https://ts.la/arthur73734 (Be sure to use it when you make your order). Tesla keeps changing the program, but here’s the latest: If you are buying a new Tesla Model 3 or Y and use my link, you will get two months of free Full Self Driving (a ~$200 value) on top of the one month that comes free with every new Model Y right now. This will get you a total of 3 months free Full Self Driving. It will be $99/month after that. For a Cybertruck, you will get $1000 off your purchase price. At this moment, Tesla is offering a year of free Supercharging with the purchase of a new Model 3 Premium or Performance.

#5788. XPENG Researchers and Academic Partners Introduce GroundingPI, An Open-Source Visual Perception Model For Physical AI

Guangzhou, China — XPENG (NYSE: XPEV, HKEX: 9868), a leading Physical AI company, has supported the development of GroundingPI, an open-source visual perception foundation model created by researchers affiliated with XPENG, Peking University, The University of Hong Kong and other academic institutions.

The research explores a new approach to embodied intelligence by focusing on a fundamental capability: enabling AI systems to accurately identify objects and understand their spatial positions in the physical world. With approximately 4 billion parameters, GroundingPI achieved the highest aggregate score among 44 baseline models evaluated across 34 perception benchmarks covering 11 capability categories.

The research paper, model weights and supporting code have been made publicly available, enabling researchers and developers to further explore perception-focused approaches to Physical AI.

As AI increasingly moves beyond digital environments into physical applications such as robotics and autonomous driving, accurately understanding the surrounding environment becomes essential.

While general-purpose foundation models have demonstrated strong capabilities in language understanding, reasoning and planning, physical intelligence requires more precise visual perception. AI systems must not only recognize objects but also identify their exact locations and spatial relationships to support real-world interaction.

GroundingPI takes a perception-focused approach to this challenge. Rather than relying on robot action labels during the perceptual model’s training, the research focuses on developing foundational visual understanding capabilities that can support downstream action-learning systems.

For example, when instructed to identify “the handle of the cup on the right”, an AI system needs to locate not only the correct cup but also the precise position of its handle. GroundingPI is designed to represent such information using spatial coordinates, including points and bounding boxes.

The model is trained across 11 categories of perceptual capabilities, including object detection, dense object localization, referring expression grounding, optical character recognition (OCR), document layout understanding, user interface element recognition and robotic spatial localization.

By strengthening these fundamental capabilities, the research explores how specialized perception models could provide a more effective visual foundation for embodied AI systems.

In evaluations conducted by the research team, GroundingPI was compared with 44 baseline models across 34 public perception benchmarks spanning 11 capability categories.

Using an aggregate evaluation score with equal weighting across the capability categories, GroundingPI achieved 73.68, compared with 71.54 for GPT-6 Astra.

The results demonstrate the potential of specialized perception-focused models to outperform larger general-purpose models on specific visual grounding tasks. The comparison relates to the evaluated perception benchmarks and does not represent a broader assessment of general AI intelligence or reasoning capabilities.

GroundingPI’s performance was particularly relevant to tasks requiring precise object localization, including complex visual scenes containing numerous objects or small targets.

These findings suggest that targeted perceptual pretraining can provide a valuable foundation for future Physical AI research, complementing the broader reasoning and planning capabilities of general-purpose foundation models.

Beyond perception benchmarks, the research team evaluated GroundingPI as a visual foundation for downstream robotic manipulation and autonomous driving tasks.

The results highlight two potential advantages for embodied intelligence.

Robotic systems often require extensive human demonstrations to learn new manipulation tasks, creating significant challenges in data collection and training.

In RoboCasa-GR1 simulation experiments, robotic learning systems using GroundingPI as their visual backbone achieved higher task success rates with 50% of the available demonstration data than the evaluated comparison models trained with 75%.

This finding suggests that stronger foundational perception capabilities may reduce the amount of action-training data required for certain robotic tasks, potentially improving the efficiency of future embodied AI development.

Another major challenge in robotics is generalization: the ability to perform a learned task when objects, backgrounds or environmental conditions change.

In RoboTwin 2.0 simulation evaluations, GroundingPI demonstrated stronger performance than the evaluated mainstream visual backbones across four out-of-distribution settings, indicating improved adaptability to unfamiliar environments.

The research team also evaluated its potential for autonomous driving perception using the nuScenes dataset. When integrated as a visual backbone, GroundingPI achieved an average open-loop trajectory prediction error of 0.296 metres in the reported evaluation.

Together, these experimental findings highlight the potential of perception-focused foundation models to support more adaptable and data-efficient embodied intelligence systems.

The research remains exploratory. Its downstream results are based on simulation and offline evaluations and do not establish readiness for real-world commercial deployment.

GroundingPI explores a research direction in which specialized visual perception capabilities complement higher-level reasoning and planning, rather than relying exclusively on general-purpose models for every component of an embodied intelligence system.

The findings provide insights into how perception-focused pretraining could contribute to future research on robotic manipulation, autonomous driving and other applications requiring precise interaction with the physical world.

To encourage further exploration and collaboration, the research team has made GroundingPI’s paper, model weights and supporting code publicly accessible, subject to the applicable open-source and third-party licensing terms.

The project represents an academic research contribution supported by XPENG and its research collaborators. The results do not constitute an announcement of integration into XPENG’s commercial products or a commitment to future deployment.

Project Website: https://groundingpi.github.io/ Research Paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39601 Source Code: https://github.com/groundingpi/GroundingPI Model Weights: https://huggingface.co/GroundingPI/GroundingPI

XPENG is a leading global Physical AI company, dedicated to bringing artificial intelligence into the physical world to reshape future mobility and smart living. Through in-house R&D, XPENG has developed a full-stack Physical AI architecture spanning Turing AI chips, world foundation models, and highly integrated software and hardware applications. This unified technology foundation of XPENG powers an expansive product portfolio of smart EVs, robotaxis, and humanoid robots, advancing the deployment of Physical AI at scale.

Headquartered in Guangzhou, China, XPENG is dual-primary listed on the New York Stock Exchange and the Hong Kong Stock Exchange. With global capabilities across R&D, manufacturing, sales, and services, XPENG drives continuous technological innovation and fosters an open Physical AI ecosystem, making life smarter, safer, and better for users worldwide. For more information, please visit https://www.xpeng.com/.

#5786. New York program funds $13 million in energy storage advancement projects

New York State’s Power Generation and Storage Innovation program has awarded nearly $13 million to 15 projects to support product development and demonstration of scalable long duration and other advanced battery energy storage solutions.

“Investing in next-generation energy storage technologies is critical to building a flexible and resilient grid that can make the most of our diverse energy resources,” said Doreen M. Harris, NYSERDA president and CEO. “These projects will advance promising solutions that can deliver power when it is needed most, strengthen grid performance and help drive down costs as we build a more affordable and reliable energy system for New York consumers.”

NYSERDA’s Power Generation and Storage Innovation program awards projects that develop and demonstrate innovative long duration energy storage (LDES) and other under-utilized, alternatives to traditional lithium-ion technologies. The awarded technologies include new designs for scalable LDES solutions that are over 10 hours in duration at rated power and will address cost, performance, and integration challenges such as grid congestion, hosting capacity constraints and lithium-ion siting limitations.

Public Service Commission Chair Rory Christian said, “Storage is poised to play an increasingly critical role in supporting a reliable, cost-effective, flexible, and lower emissions system as more clean energy resources are integrated into the grid, as well as enhance the efficiency of the state’s existing resources. NYSERDA’s dedication of these funds originating from innovation and research funding overseen by the Commission positions New York’s consumers to yield the promising long-term benefits expected by more complex and scalable applications of these technologies.”

All contracts are awarded to companies located in New York, partnered with New York companies or for development and demonstrations in the state. Today’s announcement supports NYSERDA’s Innovation and Research (I&R) program, which invests over $96 million per year to attract world-class energy innovators, reduce risk for private investors and remove barriers to clean energy adoption in New York State, leveraging $15 in additional investment for every $1 spent. NYSERDA has partnered with over 900 companies that have helped make more than 300 products commercially available for consumers, businesses and utilities.

Power Generation and Storage Innovation funding for comes from the state’s Clean Energy Fund.

#5785. Thermal Dawn — Thermal Storage Is Cheap Storage

Another great idea from Down Under: Thermal Dawn promises cost effective heating and cooling, mainly by replacing gas boilers in cold climates. CleanTechnica had a chance to interview Nick Zeniou, Founder of Thermal Dawn, recently by phone, fresh after his presentation at Everything Electric. You can hear him explain his revolutionary system here, or here with a little less drama.

Everything Electric was a good opportunity to measure public reaction to the new product. Nick tells me that hundreds of people checked out their stall. “People care about carbon cost and comfort. People don’t compromise on comfort.” Sounds like comfort trumps carbon cost. Homeowners who visited the stand and filled out the survey were focused on sustainability and cost. Nick cautions: “it was an electrification expo crowd, and 50 of the 62 already had solar, so it’s not the whole market.” See the chart below.

Although I live in the warmer part of Australia (Brisbane), many Aussies have to endure cold winters. Nick tells me he is looking at installs in Sydney, Canberra, and Melbourne in the deep south. Thermal Dawn can store heat up to 70 degrees Celsius, or down to 5 degrees. Thermal Dawns’ equipment can run cold or hot water through pipes and radiators that are already installed, depending on the need — without having to run an expensive gas boiler. He describes it as a thermal battery. Just to be clear, it does not generate electricity.

Check out this graph below, which shows the Thermal Dawn system in action at a house in Melbourne during a day in August 2026. It shows room temperature and when the heat pump was running, in 15-minute samples, unedited. The heat pump ran until about 1:45pm, then not again, heated by stored energy through the evening peak.

“What about return on investment,” I asked. He described two systems they have had running over the southern Australian winter which have saved the homeowners approximately AU$1,500 each. “The first was a house in Melbourne, our first install, live since May 2026. The owners charge the system in their retailer’s free three-hour midday window, so the heat for the evening costs them close to nothing. The second was a Warragul (slab-heated home, Victoria): installed in two days in July 2026. Our controls turn the home’s concrete slab into part of the battery, managing when it charges and when it releases heat. On 95% of winter evenings, the heat pumps did not run at all between 5 pm and 9pm. Since commissioning, 70,521 MJ of gas has been avoided by just these two installations.”

Nick further explains: “A standard heat pump draws from the grid during those expensive morning and evening hours. You’ve escaped gas, but you’re still paying premium rates every time the temperature drops at night. Thermal Dawn is different. Our system charges when power is cheapest, a free midday window, your own solar, or late-night off-peak, then delivers that stored warmth through your radiators and underfloor heating all evening, with the compressor off through the expensive hours.

It appears that Thermal Dawn’s secret sauce is “Phase Change Materials.” Naturally, Nick declined to share exactly what Thermal Dawn is using. The system replaces a gas boiler with a combination of reverse cycle heat pump, well insulated water tank, and phase change materials. PCMs are designed to freeze or melt at a specific temperature, storing and releasing heat when they change physical state, usually from solid to liquid. “PCM doubles our heat capacity.” The heating applications I could understand, what about the cooling? “Cool water from the tank runs through a fan coil unit, and the fan blows cool air into the room. Cooling is an optional add-on.” That’s the answer.

How do thermal storage and a home battery/solar system work together? “Thermal storage is cheap storage doing one job very well. Heating, cooling, and hot water are the largest, most predictable loads in the house, and covering them thermally is far cheaper per kWh than covering them from lithium. A battery is expensive storage doing a general-purpose job.

“The practical effect is that your battery stops being the thing that carries your heating. It’s freed up for lights, appliances, the EV, and overnight backup, and it lasts longer for not being cycled hard every winter evening. Homes with a battery already are often the clearest case, because the expensive storage stops being spent on the cheapest possible job.”

Thermal dawn connects to existing hydronic pipework. The gas boiler with its high gas bills is gone. Check out the animation of heat discharge overnight. “The thermal battery charged on solar during the day and now heats the house while the heat pump stays off. It’s an illustration of what the app shows, not a recording of a live home.” For those rooms that don’t have hydronic radiators or underfloor slab heating, Thermal Dawn installs fan coil units. Nick tells me via a followup email that: “It’s a small indoor unit with a water coil and a fan: warm water from the thermal battery runs through the coil, and the fan blows the heating or cooling into the room.”

The heavy technical discussion out of the way, I asked Nick about his background. He grew up in an isolated part of New Zealand. His job was to collect the firewood for the family’s heat source — a fireplace. He had to get the fire going by 4:00 pm or the house would not warm up for the cold evening. Timing was very important, as it is for electricity use.

Nick is a mechanical engineer who has worked across tech companies and startups. While working on installing commercial air conditioning, he came across the idea that became Thermal Dawn. Nick joined the dots between the huge variations in electricity supply cost throughout each 24-hour period, with an average home’s thermal energy needs (up to two-thirds of consumption) and the fact that homes are becoming power stations. Thence to thermal batteries powered by Thermal Dawn technology.

He reminisces about the start of the business: “When our first unit was finished, we loaded it into a ute at 5pm and I drove it down the Hume overnight, Sydney to Melbourne (1000 km), to install it the next morning. We had a four-month-old at home at the time. My wife Linda is my co-founder and leads mechanical design and manufacturing, so while I was on the road, she was looking after a newborn.”

For those who would like to get involved, Nick invites Australian homeowners to sign up here and they’ll be in touch. “Readers overseas: send us a message or request a quote at the same link to register interest. If we hear from enough people in one area, we can bring forward our plans to come there.”

Having proven their point, Thermal Dawn is cleared to sell and installing its first batch now, with a larger product for ducted air conditioning in development. I asked about apartment block heating and Nick confirmed that they are looking at it but want to get the domestic home sorted first.

I asked a couple of followup questions “What about the Victorian government’s position?” Nick’s answer:  “Victoria is set to bring in support for systems like ours from the middle of next year. With the state election in November, there’s talk of a change in direction and of belt-tightening, so we’ve built Thermal Dawn to stand on its own. It saves households money without a subsidy, and any support is a bonus.”

Any plans for installs in Canberra? “Yes. Canberra is one of the best places in Australia for this: cold winters, plenty of hydronic homes, and a territory already moving off gas. We’re talking with installers there now.”

I really appreciate these technological experts when they take the time to explain things to me. Sometimes I feel like Winnie the Pooh — a bear of very little brain. Looks like Thermal Dawn has solved another part of the energy puzzle — good news for homeowners and also for the planet.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5784. Inside the ‘pressure cooker’: Electric utility regulators gather in wake of electoral earthquake

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Newly elected leaders and customers are “questioning our foundational regulatory approaches,” said Ann Rendahl, president of the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners.

Ann Rendahl, president of the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners, did not mince words while addressing her peers Sunday in San Juan, Puerto Rico, where NARUC is holding its annual meeting in the wake of a midterm election where concern over electricity costs helped Democrats surge to power in Congress and many states.

“Our newly elected leaders and customers just want rates to go down,” said Rendahl, a state regulator in Washington.

“They don't really care how we do it,” but they are “questioning our foundational regulatory approaches,” she added.

Rendahl was speaking at the first meeting, held on the sidelines of NARUC’s main conference, of a new initiative by state regulators, ratepayer advocates and investor-owned utilities aimed at developing approaches for evaluating long-term grid investments while delivering measurable value to consumers. The effort is led by the Critical Consumer Issues Forum, which includes NARUC members, as well as representatives from the National Association of State Utility Consumer Advocates and the Edison Electric Institute, a trade group for investor-owned utilities.

The midterm election results last week will affect state energy policy in different ways, according to speakers at the meeting.

It also could have implications for relations between states and the federal government, which have become fraught in some places over Department of Energy interventions — including ordering coal plants to run past their planned retirement dates.

In September, a federal appeals court ruled that the DOE’s “emergency” order requiring the J.H. Campbell plant in Michigan to remain operational was illegal, noting that state regulators and the Midcontinent grid operator signed off on the plant’s retirement.

“It's this fight between who does resource adequacy — that state role, that federal role — and everything in between, " said Michael Moody, NASUCA president and Michigan assistant attorney general.

Moody said he hopes the midterm results will spur more federal-state cooperation.

In Arizona, the state’s House and Senate flipped to Democratic control, while two Democrats were elected to the previously all-Republican Arizona Corporation Commission.

Lea Márquez Peterson, the longest-tenured ACC commissioner, who was not on the ballot, predicted the state’s new legislative majority will take up energy policy.

“I think we're going to see a lot of bills come out that will influence our world,” she said.

The midterm results will also likely increase the differences between states on energy policy, said Jeff Lyng, Xcel Energy’s chief sustainability officer and vice president of external affairs and policy.

“I think we're going to see a bit more of that patchwork,” he said. “But ultimately, [while] the words that we use in certain jurisdictions may be different … the strategy fundamentally doesn't change.”

Ana Ortega, commissioner on the Florida Public Service Commission, warned about pressure from the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission for increased regional transmission.

“Florida is a large peninsular state, and it's got all of its load at the bottom,” she said. “So, if you're looking to regionalize, it doesn't necessarily make sense for us. I'm not saying that we're not complying, but it doesn't [make sense].”

“We're all in the pressure cooker,” and concerns about data centers have added to the heat, Márquez Peterson said.

Options for balancing the need for grid investments with affordability include maximizing the value of the grid through grid-enhancing technologies and energy efficiency, she said.

In the face of affordability issues, Moody said utilities need to prioritize their spending plans, evaluate lower-cost alternatives and present strong cost/benefit analysis.

“Sometimes, there's these great ideas that have to be pushed off,” he said.

However, Ortega cautioned that delaying grid investments can sometimes be more costly in the long run.

“You have to also think about the opportunity costs of not planning and not investing in the grid,” she said. “I know that's unpopular. I know it's hard, but in some ways, it's better for long-term customer value.”

Load growth can help reduce customer costs, but “growth has to pay for growth,” according to Lyng. A gigawatt of hyperscaler load has the potential to put about $1 billion of downward pressure on rates over a 15 or 20 years, he said.

Lyng also said that investing in renewable energy, especially wind, has helped Xcel keep residential rates that are roughly 30% below the national average.

Xcel’s customers have saved about $6 billion on wind energy, with another $12 billion in savings expected by the end of this decade, he said.

However, amid shifting priorities, Xcel may not meet its commitment to be net-zero by 2050, a goal set in 2018, according to Lyng.

Now, sustainability plays “fourth fiddle” to safety, reliability and affordability, he said.

“We are not walking away from the [sustainability] goals,” Lyng said. “We still have those commitments, but we have to move at the speed of value. We have to move in a disciplined way. We can't force a clean energy transition.”

Still, Xcel sees a “very clear pathway” to achieving individual state clean energy targets, he said.
