#5783. How Can Europe Accelerate Electric Trucking?

Europe is having a hard time lately, with skyrocketing oil prices dampening economies. Fuel costs, particularly diesel, are prompting surging interest in truck electrification. Europe has been urging truck makers to move toward electric trucking, and they have responded with a variety of electric semi trucks from many manufacturers. Europe is a group of sovereign countries with many standards. The European Union is a political and economic union of 27 states in that area. It issued directives urging action and imposing penalties. In response to European Union directives, companies have formed EV100, a coalition of corporations responding to the need. In a recent story, Steve Hanley wrote about European electric truck makers’ difficulties expanding truck electrification. Here’s a quotation from the story: “In a letter to EU Commission president Ursula von der Leyen, of primary concern, in their estimation, is expanding the charging infrastructure for heavy-duty trucks in Europe.” There is no shortage of electric trucks in Europe. Expansion could go faster were it not for charging infrastructure. Manufacturers complain about “an alarming delay” in conditions needed to drive market uptake. For Europe, truck electrification is not working as well as in China, where over 90% of the world’s electric trucks reside. The differences between China and the EU show some reasons why truck electrification is slower in Europe. How did China succeed where the EU failed? China made electric trucks a freight system, not a vehicle category.

Power is a major factor in electric grid infrastructure. Charging electric trucks places demands on power. Peak power determines how much infrastructure is necessary. In grid planning, peak annual power determines how much capital equipment is necessary. Reducing peak power demand is a critical factor for electric truck infrastructure. Figure 1 shows one method of reducing power called route stuffing. It schedules departures on heavy traffic corridors at regularly spaced intervals. Corridors like ones between ports and warehouses are a target for those practices. These corridors often route trucks in a round trip chain. Filling routes with scheduled trips can reduce power demand compared to random trip scheduling. The article Battery Swap Uses Fewer Batteries Than You Think is devoted to methods of analysis and planning that can lead to lower peak power demand. There are other benefits to planning, such as greater truck load capacity from the same amount of batteries. Part of the solution to slow electric truck infrastructure development is to plan infrastructure and truck use practices to reduce the charging power burden. Given the pressing and enduring need due to the diesel fuel cost crisis, it is necessary to reprioritize grid power upgrade demands. Demands for data center power upgrades like transformers for substations should be re shuffled and prioritized toward the diesel crisis.

In two articles, Michael Barnard outlined the issues. Vehicles are not a transportation system. What does Europe have now? It has an EU Commission interacting with a coalition, EV100. The problem is, there are plenty of trucks available, but who is in charge of infrastructure? You can’t have electric trucks without means of charging, but as the coalition complains, “Significant improvements to charging infrastructure, a reduction in carbon dioxide based tolls, and a coherent policy framework are urgently needed.” There are so many stakeholders — including the commission, vehicles makers, and charging companies across multiple countries — that no one is in charge of infrastructure, and without it, nothing moves. It needs a unity of purpose and action.

If China is succeeding, why not adopt its strategy? Perhaps the answer is right in front of everyone’s nose. As Michael puts it, “the policy connects vehicles to corridors, depots, service areas, grids and freight use cases instead of treating sales targets as sufficient.” CATL offered Europe a system, not a vehicle. This is a “turn key” solution. It’s a well thought out system, including use of methods and practices, not an anything goes free-for-all. That kind of stability and predictability is what EV100 leaders are looking for. This is not a swap-or-nothing scenario. Different techniques apply to various use cases. Swap must be planned for high-use freight corridors and other proper applications. Swap trucks can also fast charge or slow charge, providing flexibility and a system of trucks that includes smaller trucks, not just semis.

What does all this have to do with Europe’s slowly advancing infrastructure? Simply put, electric trucks put heavy charging demands on the grid. Trucks can have megawatt-hour batteries that require megawatt power charging to charge in an hour. Reducing grid power demands can reduce grid infrastructure upgrade costs, and leverage capital investment toward faster implementation.

“The process of getting megawatt charging equipment connected to the grid can take several years, delaying deployment even where operators and infrastructure providers are ready to invest.” The need for megawatt charging equipment is reduced by accelerating swap network development instead of fixed battery trucks that must be charged by fast chargers. “Swaptopus is easy to misread as a quirky name for another electric-truck infrastructure project. It is a newly formed joint venture between Octopus Energy and CATL, created to develop automated battery-swapping hubs for heavy trucks across Europe.” Unlike the government of China that played a role in encouraging standards and cooperation in infrastructure, Europe has a looser arrangement between many countries with various standards. Europe is learning the slow, hard way where battery swapping is most needed.

Every truck application has different optimization. It means that there must be different approaches to fit freight operations in heavy traffic corridors. Quoting Michael Barnard, “Freight operators often run repeated routes between ports, factories, distribution centres and logistics hubs. Those patterns provide known traffic, known stopping points and relatively predictable electricity demand. A swap hub can be placed around an existing freight flow instead of hoping that enough unrelated trucks eventually pass through.”

Freight corridors are a scenario made to order for battery swap. Real trucking solutions include a mix of vehicle and infrastructure types — from small, local delivery, to large, long-haul freight; and from slow, overnight charging, to medium power charging and battery swap — all used in concert to accelerate heavy truck electrification. With a wide variety of charging options, and vehicles and charging use cases targeted in coordination to avoid peak power demands, the path forward is eased. School buses don’t need expensive high-power fast charging. Lower-power chargers can do the trick. Local delivery can use a mix of overnight and medium power charging. Heavy truck dispatch must be more carefully coordinated to avoid peak demand and grid upgrade costs. A mix of medium-power charging and swap stations along with planned use cases can successfully lower electric freight power needs and transport system capital expenses to speed transport electrification.

Transformation and conversion of diesel freight systems to electric requires coordination and planning between government agencies and coalitions of corporations, cities, government agencies, and planners. Transportation is a system basic to societies and economics. Coordination between groups at all levels results in practices, use cases, and infrastructure that produce benefits for all. Grid expansion and construction can take years. In order for Europe to implement truck freight electrification, more structure and planning is needed to minimize grid power requirements, speed implementation, and maximize capital investment results. Transportation system, practices, and infrastructure must be planned for optimum results.

has studied wind, electric vehicles, and environmental issues. An electrical engineer familiar with power and electronics, he has participated in the Automotive X Prize contest. He is an avid writer, specializing in electric vehicles, batteries, and wind energy.

#5782. PPL increases capital plan to $25B with proposed Kentucky gas plant

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The company projects some 50 GW of potential load growth, mostly from data centers, across Kentucky and Pennsylvania.

PPL Corp. raised its base capital plan to $25 billion for 2027 through 2030 — $2 billion more than the prior plan, primarily due to a proposed combined-cycle gas turbine plant in Kentucky, President and CEO Vincent Sorgi said during a Friday third-quarter earnings call.

If approved by regulators, the new plant would have 645 MW of capacity, and be expected to reach commercial operations in the second quarter of 2032, according to PPL’s earnings presentation.

In Kentucky, where PPL operates Louisville Gas and Electric and Kentucky Utilities, the company’s spending is set to increase from $2.3 billion in 2026 to $3.6 billion in 2027.

“I personally think we're going to be building generation for the foreseeable future in Kentucky, just given what we see with the quarter-over-quarter movement in the pipeline,” Sorgi said during the call.

Regarding the Kentucky Public Service Commission’s previous denials of PPL’s requests to build new gas capacity, Sorgi said, “Of course, the commission is going to be diligent in their review of all of that, but conservatism is not cost-free.”

“Sometimes, being overly conservative in regulatory outcomes ends up costing our customers, and we've seen that in the past,” he continued.

“I clearly want to try to stave that off in this next [Certificate of Public Convenience and Necessity],” which is anticipated in the first quarter of 2027, he added.

Sorgi said that the next “next CPCN filing will occur once we have signed a data center [electric service agreement]” in the state.

Kentucky’s development pipeline “has expanded by 1 GW to 14.7 GW of potential load growth through 2032,” with data center-related load representing 12.8 GW, according to PPL’s earnings presentation.

PPL also continues to see a strong data center pipeline in Pennsylvania, where projects in “advanced stages” increased for the 11th consecutive quarter to 35.3 GW, up 3.5 GW from the second quarter, Sorgi said.

Sorgi said the company is seeing “significant progress” in converting that pipeline into committed projects.

“Projects with a probability of success of 50% or greater have increased to 4 GW of additional peak demand, with the average probability for these projects sitting at almost 85%,” he said.

Invitium Energy – which PPL owns 51% of in a joint venture with Blackstone – signed a letter of intent on Friday with QTS Data Centers “to be their generation solution provider in portions of [Pennsylvania],” said PPL’s earnings presentation. Blackstone also owns QTS.

“Invitium has agreed to all key commercial terms with QTS providing for ~2GW of generation capacity to supply power to QTS’s Salem Township data center project with a 20-year initial term with multiple five-year renewals permitted,” the presentation said.

The proposed generation portfolio “provides for two combined cycle natural gas plants complemented with fuel cells and battery storage,” it said.

The structure of the deal “provides a regulated-like risk profile with fixed monthly payments (with annual escalation) covering return of and on invested capital plus fixed operating costs and variable payments when the plants run, covering fuel and variable operating costs,” the presentation added.

Sorgi expressed confidence about the company’s data center-related business in response to an analyst’s question about the potential impact of the midterm election results.

“The national election results could actually support our pipelines that we have both in Pennsylvania and Kentucky,” he said. “Both of our governors have signed executive orders for developing data centers and doing it the right way.”

He suggested that restrictions on data centers in other states could be a net benefit to PPL.

“I do think some of the election results could, in some other states, potentially have a chilling impact, which could drive some of those projects or future development projects to both Pennsylvania and Kentucky,” Sorgi said.

#5781. Arizona’s longest-tenured utility commissioner on large loads, affordability and Western markets

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“We have been talking for years that growth must pay for growth, and I think customers are asking for proof [of that],” Lea Márquez Peterson told Utility Dive on the sidelines of NARUC’s meeting.

Utility Dive sat down with Lea Márquez Peterson, a commissioner on the Arizona Corporation Commission, on Sunday at the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners’ annual meeting in San Juan, Puerto Rico, to hear about her views on the challenges facing the ACC and other state regulators.

Márquez Peterson was appointed to the ACC in 2019 by the governor and won elections for four-year terms in 2020 and 2024 — making her the longest tenured commissioner at the agency today.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

UTILITY DIVE: It seems like affordability and data centers emerged as top issues at NARUC’s annual meeting two years ago. To what extent have those issues changed since then?

Lea Márquez Peterson: I think in Arizona, we are growing so rapidly, and we're seeing a lot of large load requests. But that's not just data centers — it's semiconductors. And so we're having conversations, probably a little more in depth and more publicly than we did two years ago, with water use leading the conversation.

The top thing, when you come to Arizona to build anything, is you tell us about your water use, and the public is very sensitive to this. They've seen a lot of requests and development projects come through our state, and not all have been as transparent as they should be.

What about concerns that large loads are causing rates to go up?

We saw that play out in this last election cycle. We have been talking for years that growth must pay for growth, and I think customers are asking for proof [of that]. What are the components of cost? How are you structuring this as a commission, and how do we ensure that growth is really paying for growth? And so you see that playing out across the nation, whether it's large-load tariffs, or, in [Arizona], energy supply agreements.

Has the commission set any rules around those agreements?

They evolved over time. I think they were simpler years ago, and as more and more load comes through … the utilities are getting even more sophisticated in how they're handling it.

Is there any interest in standardizing it with a large-load tariff?

Not statewide. We have our upcoming large rate cases coming up, and I think you'll see some development of their large load activity in those rate cases.

What levers does the commission have on affordability?

It could come to rate design, how that's structured. It's looking at the supply agreements. How much of the cost allocation should a large load be covering? I think we have a lot of different tools. Are the [large loads] going to be partners in some of the new generation?

I've been defined in the media as skeptical. I’d stick with that. We passed it as a policy statement in Arizona, and I thought it was interesting conceptually, but I wanted proof that it really was in the best interest for customers, and so I'm kind of working through that case by case as they come before us. We have [Tuscon Electric Power] before us next week and we have [Arizona Public Service] in mid-December.

On market development in the West, Arizona's big utilities are opting for the Southwest Power Pool’s Markets+. With Bonneville potentially switching to the Enhanced Day-Ahead Market, you asked the utilities to provide updated information about their decision. So how are you thinking about market development and opportunities for the Arizona utilities?

The ACC doesn’t have a choice [on which market the utilities join], but I wanted to better understand how that impacts customers — the exiting of the Energy Imbalance Market — whatever regional markets are decided, and I wanted to understand the [cost/benefit] analysis that was done by [the utilities].

I'm going to follow up with a workshop on Feb. 3 where I want to ask different questions on the components of those questions I asked in the letter, and then have some stakeholders ask questions, too … and just make sure that we're all aware. There’s no reason there can’t be transparency.

I want to just see that the benefits, whatever market they choose, is in the best interest of ratepayers. It makes a difference — whether from a reliability perspective, transmission availability, resource planning, whatever it is. I want to better understand the benefits.

How comfortable are you with Arizona’s generating resource situation?

I supported an “all the above” approach. Reliability is so key. That means you cannot have power outages, especially during the heat of summer.

I'm very focused on nuclear, but it's very expensive. About 30% of the state is served by the Palo Verde nuclear generating station. So we might see new nuclear in the future, but who's going to pay for that? What role do large load users have, perhaps?

Looking ahead to next year, what are the top issues that you and the ACC will be dealing with?

Next year, we will have voted on a number of formula rate cases. We will have annual true-ups, and I think there's a lot to explore and develop with those true-ups. Was there enough analysis done? Was there enough time for the [ACC] staff to do the analysis? How much could stakeholders weigh in?

PJM had planned to accept offers for backstop power supplies from Sept. 30 through Oct. 21 after falling short of its reserve margin targets. But FERC only partially approved its plan, zeroing in on cost allocation concerns.

#5780. Trump Is Gonna Cave On Offshore Wind In 5…4…3…

…JK! US President Donald Trump will never cry surrender in the war against offshore wind turbines. The President was burned once before, when a wind farm arose in Aberdeen Bay in Scotland, within view of a Trump-branded golf course back in the early 2000’s. Never again! The President has taken extreme measures to prevent such blight from tainting the shores of the United States. Nevertheless, scores of the pesky oversized pinwheels have already eluded the Trump chopper, and many more are on the way.

The latest news concerns CVOW, the 2.6-gigwatt Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind project under the wing of the leading utility Dominion. As the name of the project indicates, CVOW sits in the Atlantic Ocean about 24 nautical miles (28 land miles) off the coast of Virginia Beach, Virginia.

CVOW began taking shape during Trump’s first turn around the Oval Office, and construction was well under way when his second term began on January 20, 2025.

Immediately upon taking office, Trump abruptly suspended the offshore wind lease program administered by the Department of the Interior. He also ordered spurious re-reviews of offshore projects that were already in the permitting pipeline, and he had the Department of Justice administer a legally questionable buy-back program to prevent activity from stirring among other lease holders. In addition, he had the Interior Department issue multiple emergency orders aimed at stopping five offshore projects off the coasts of Rhode Island, Connecticut, New Jersey, New York, Maryland, and Massachusetts, where construction work was already well under way.

So far, though, only one part of the effort has stuck. Last year a federal judge affirmed that any President, Trump or not, has the authority to stop issuing new offshore leases. However, the same judge decided there is no blanket authority to suspend offshore leases that are already in the permitting process. The buy-back program is also in litigation, with critics raising questions about the funds used to pay off lease holders among other issues.

Additionally,  the Interior Department failed to convince multiple federal judges that the emergency orders had any merit. Work has continued on all five Atlantic coast projects, including CVOW.

CVOW began delivering its first kilowatts to shore in March, and Dominion recapped its progress on the wind farm in its Q3 report on October 30. As handily summarized by the news organization Maritime Executive, the project currently clocks in at 85% complete. All of the main turbine components — nacelles, towers, and blades — for the wind farm’s 176 offshore wind turbines have been fabricated. Forty-four have been fully installed so far. With a 45th coming any day now, the wind farm currently hosts 640 megawatts out of the 2,600 megawatt total.

Since Trump declared an “Energy Emergency” in January of 2025, it’s fair to ask why he has spent so much time, energy and taxpayer dollars to crush an entire domestic energy industry. After all, if one was not reading too closely between the lines of the declaration, the new energy policy is little more than a rehash of former President Barack Obama’s “All Of The Above” energy policy. As further embellished by Energy Secretary Chris Wright, the new version specifically taps multiple zero and low-emission energy resources for support including nuclear energy, biomass, hydropower, and geothermal energy, just like Obama did, along the emerging field of marine energy, which was barely a speck on the horizon while Obama was in office.

Energy storage, and the critical minerals needed in the clean tech industry, have also been gathered up in a Trumpian embrace.

Conspicuously absent, however, are the two most abundant, economical, and easily accessible domestic energy resources, namely, wind and solar.

If there really was an energy emergency…oh, never mind! Despite Trump’s complaints, offshore wind is a mature industry with a vigorous supply global supply chain and support equipment, as deftly illustrated by the CVOW project. In the October 30 report, Dominion noted that it takes only about two days for an offshore construction vessel to install a wind turbine on its foundation.

While the offshore wind construction timetable is subject to weather delays, Dominion also stated that the project is on schedule — despite any further delays related to last year’s stop-work order.

Speaking of the stop-work order, CVOW was conspicuously excluded from previous rounds of stop-work orders impacting the four other Atlantic coast wind farms under construction in 2025. If you’re guessing that Virginia’s Republican Governor, Glenn Youngkin, had a hand in that decision, drop a note in the discussion thread. Youngkin publicly supported the CVOW project but he was term-limited out of office last year. A stop-work order would have put an embarrassing crimp in his next-step political plans. The order came down only after Election Day 2025, after the Democratic candidate, Abigail Spanberger, won the post.

In a classic case of karma biting back, CVOW is a  more efficient and economical project than initially envisioned. A round of delays during Trump’s first term in office gave Dominion an opportunity to redesign the project with next-generation wind turbines. Those delays also enabled Dominion to take advantage of a new generation of turbine installation vessels that are purpose-built to cut offshore construction costs.

The results speak for themselves. In its October 30 report, Dominion indicated that CVOW costs were still consistent with its capital budget, even accounting for the cost of last year’s stop-work order and costs related to Trump’s tariff wars.

“The estimates on the levelized cost of energy (LCOE) also continue to fall,” Maritime Executive summarized. “After starting with a forecasted range of $80-$90 per MWh, and adjustments for changes after the change in administrations, they have lowered the LCOE to $62, down from an estimate of $83 presented in August” (LCOE is a calculation that provides a common ground for comparing costs among different forms of power generation).

Maritime Executive further reported that Dominion expects to save its ratepayers about $5 billion on fuel over the first 10 years of operation.

Trump’s war on wind has also petered out elsewhere. The Revolution Wind project off the coasts of Connecticut and Massachusetts has also been a target of  multiple stop-work orders over the past two years, with the latest hammer coming down just two weeks ago, when the 704-megawatt wind farm was almost complete.

Revolution Wind is now fully operational, joining the nearby Vineyard Wind project which also endured multiple attacks from Trump. As reported by public radio station WBUR, the savings from Revolution Wind is expected to ripple out through New England, saving $500 million for ratepayers in the region every year.

As for the domestic offshore wind industry writ large, the US lost thousands of onshore supply chain jobs all around the US in addition to job losses related to portside and offshore operation. However, the trade organization Oceantic Network is already prepping for a comeback. “The road to recovery for the embattled American offshore wind industry starts today with a new political mandate to tackle the affordability crisis,” Oceantic President and CEO Liz said in a public statement celebrating the Republican Party’s crushing defeat in the 202y midterm elections.

Photo: The offshore wind construction vessel Charybdis can install wind turbines at Virginia’s CVOW wind farm in just two days (cropped; courtesy of Dominion via CleanTechnica archive).

#5778. Utilities should consider ‘taking a breather’ from rate cases: Minnesota PUC vice chair

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“Filing rate cases right now is really painful,” Joseph Sullivan told Utility Dive in an interview. He also discussed his concerns with overly complex data center cost allocation.

Utility Dive met with Minnesota Public Utilities Commission Vice Chair Joseph Sullivan on Monday at the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners’ annual meeting in San Juan, Puerto Rico.

We wanted to hear his perspective on the top challenges facing regulators in Minnesota, home to utilities like Minnesota Power, Otter Tail Power and Xcel Energy’s Northern States Power-Minnesota.

Sullivan joined the PUC in April 2020, and was reappointed by Gov. Tim Walz in January to a second six-year term.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

UTILITY DIVE: What are the top challenges you're facing at the commission?

Joseph Sullivan: Certainly affordability. In recent rate cases, it's abundantly clear that many people are struggling to pay their bills, and they're going to struggle to pay a new higher bill.

On top of that, you have the compounding impact of the system that's changing. We have a system that is getting older. We have older power plants that need to be replaced. We have clean energy targets and requirements in our state.

And then you put on top of that new large loads that are coming, so we have transmission lines that are coming in. Many people are looking at these transmission lines, and they say, "Why is this transmission line going in? Is it going to increase the bills that I pay?”

In addition, people don't trust the institutions. That's becoming more and more evident and more difficult. You can see that in our hearings, which are becoming more contentious. What we receive in the dockets and the record is becoming more and more contentious, and in some cases, very worrisome.

How important is transparency in terms of rate cases, large loads?

Transparency has certainly been an issue with some of the data centers that have been proposed. People are concerned about what is happening within their community.

I would say, though, it's not just transparency that's an issue. It's context. People oftentimes just don't understand how the system works, and why would they?

But when you add that lack of transparency, plus the lack of contextual knowledge about the system, plus a degradation of faith in the institutions — it ends up being a really foul mix.

On the affordability issue, what can the commission do?

The biggest affordability program that our state has are our demand side management programs. They are taking millions of megawatt hours out of the system every year, and they're making us more productive. If you put those megawatt hours back in the system, our system would be about 20% larger. So that is a very real savings to people. All ratepayers benefit because we have to build fewer power plants and transmission lines. So, that's one of our main go-tos.

We have put a lot of work into setting up programs that our gas utilities run and our electric utilities run to help people avoid being disconnected, to help them with the arrearages.

When our legislature passed data center legislation last year, they put in a component that the data centers would have to pay into the state's weatherization fund.

What about things within a rate case: return on equity, debt to equity ratios?

Why that didn't spring immediately to mind is because that's what we do. Every rate case is about prudency. Is this expenditure just and reasonable, and therefore prudent? We focus like a laser beam on that.

Under our state statutes and under my thinking of how our law is, you don't include affordability in that ROE determination.

Is there anything that the utilities could be doing better to meet some of these challenges?

This is going to be really hard for them, but filing rate cases right now is really painful. I recognize that they have capital needs. But taking a breather from rate cases could be very helpful. We've had a lot of rate cases in the last year. They come at a — politically — really not great time.

Things that they could do also would be to be supportive of affordability programs that are going to be helpful to keeping people connected or paying down arrearages. I want the utilities to be creative in how they are addressing affordability.

The utilities also have to really clearly articulate that they are primarily investing in resources without fuel clauses, that don't have a fuel impact. Like, “We're investing in resources that are less expensive in other parts of your bill.”

And keeping rate increases to below the rate of inflation.

What effect do you think the midterms may have on state energy policy (In Minnesota, Democrats maintained control of the governor’s seat and Senate and took full control of the House in the midterm elections)?

For six of the last eight years, we've basically been on Democratic control. We already have a 100% carbon-free statute. We have our new energy efficiency framework. A lot of these big pieces are already set in stone, and we're in implementation mode on them.

The discussion in Minnesota will certainly be on data centers. Data center moratorium legislation was offered last year, and I think it'll be offered again this year. The politics has definitely changed, and some of those initiatives may have more momentum.

For me, the interesting question will be do the advocates who are more interested in small solar, are they on the ascendancy? Or are the people who are supportive of the vertically integrated model, are they on the ascendancy? Restructuring versus continuing the regulated approach — where does that land?

How are things going with the Midcontinent Independent System Operator?

In general, MISO is doing a good job with juggling between a lot of stakeholders.

We've continued on the pathway with the [Long-Range Transmission Planning] Tranche One [transmission projects]. Minnesota is done with our permitting for Tranche One. We're deep into our permitting for Tranche 2.1. So MISO has been moving forward — not as fast as I'd like — approving new transmission.

What I am worried about with MISO right now is the cost allocation for large loads, and in particular, I'm concerned that MISO has developed, along with the transmission owners, a very complicated cost allocation framework. We don't understand how it is consistent with not just Minnesota state law, but with what the Trump administration has said [on ratepayer protection], with where it appears that Congress is going, with making sure that data centers are paying both their incremental and their embedded costs.

#5777. Sierra Club: We Cannot Support the Permitting Bill As Currently Written

Democrats have been given a mandate to fight for a better deal for the American people.

Washington, DC — In the wake of this week’s overwhelming blue wave election, the Senate may still take a vote during the lame duck session on the “Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026” permitting bill.

While the bill contains provisions that would help boost clean energy and lower energy bills, it also includes troubling provisions that profoundly weaken key environmental protections and limit communities’ ability to have a say in what happens in their neighborhoods.

The Sierra Club cannot support this legislation without fundamental changes. Congressional Democrats must demand the following:

Sierra Club Executive Director Loren Blackford released the following statement:

“The Sierra Club supports updating and expediting transmission and clean energy permitting, and have long advocated for a bill that will lower costs, reduce air, water, and climate pollution, protect communities, and expand America’s grid. As written, this bill achieves critical wins on transmission and potential emissions reduction. However, this does not justify abandoning Tribal consultation, clean water safeguards, and wildlife protections, nor further enabling a rogue president that is waging a war on clean energy. As currently drafted, this bill will hand more power to the executive branch, erode democratic norms, and cause environmental harm to our water, wildlife, and public health protections far beyond the scope of federal permitting. The Sierra Club cannot support this bill as currently written.

“The American people sent a clear message that they are fed up with a lawless president who raises costs with his chaotic and vindictive energy policy. They want a congress that will act on behalf of their interests, not the corporate polluters Donald Trump and Congressional Republicans have prioritized. The current Republican Congressional leadership, which passed the largest attack on affordable clean energy in American history and stood by as Trump waged his expensive and illegal war on clean energy, will be out of power in a matter of weeks.

“Senate Democrats have been given a mandate to act decisively to deliver relief on energy bills and level the playing field for clean energy. They can and must fight for a better deal.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. #5776. Most generators prepared for extreme cold, but risks remain: NERC

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Despite improvements to cold-weather operations, the grid “continues to face serious reliability risks during the winter period,” NERC President and CEO Jim Robb said.

Almost all U.S. power generators are capable of operating in extreme cold, with a relative few facing known constraints that could keep them from reinforcing their ability to produce electricity during winter storms, the North American Electric Reliability Corp. said Thursday.

“Industry is rising to the challenge of protecting the grid during periods of extreme cold,” NERC President and CEO Jim Robb said in a statement.

The reliability watchdog last month filed its “Cold Weather Data Annual Report” with the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission, tallying the industry’s progress in implementing new winter weatherization requirements. About 94% of the bulk power system’s total capacity could be called on to operate below 32 degrees, thus NERC requires those units to implement protective measures. Relatively few units experienced reliability issues requiring corrective action plans, NERC said.

Units constituting 4.3% of capacity are implementing corrective action plans to address reliability issues, and 60% of those plans will be completed before this upcoming winter, NERC said. “A small percentage of units,” about 7.4% of the total capacity, “declared a constraint that precluded them from implementing corrective actions.”

The findings “highlight measurable improvements in the electric industry’s cold weather preparedness, while underscoring the need for continued vigilance to address winter reliability risks,” NERC said.

The risk from extended corrective action plans and cold weather constraints “is limited,” NERC said. “Based on the data reported this year, 99% of the total net winter capacity in the continental U.S. responded that they were able to operate” at calculated extreme cold weather temperatures.

Generator owners reported a total net winter capacity of 1,154,231 MW in the continental United States. That’s a 7% increase over the winter 2024-2025 reporting period, NERC said. Owners reported cold weather constraints for 633 units comprising 85,691 MW, with known limitations of wind accounting for 71% of units reporting a constraint.

“Coupled with our recent joint report with FERC on winter system performance, it’s clear that improved preparation is making a difference with no significant system disruptions over the past winter,” Robb said. “While that is good news, the bulk power system continues to face serious reliability risks during the winter period.”

Milder temperatures in some regions could provide relief this winter as well. The El Niño weather pattern is likely to result in warmer-than-average temperatures in the northern U.S., according to the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration. Wetter-than-average conditions are likely across the southern U.S., from California to Florida, and along the East Coast, NOAA said.

Cold weather grid operating standards have been a priority for FERC and NERC since Winter Storm Uri in 2021, when about 4.5 million Texans lost power because power plants were unable to run.

PJM Interconnection, the grid operator for 13 states and the District of Columbia, on Thursday said it expects to have 182,426 MW of operational capacity to serve a forecasted peak electricity demand of approximately 147,500 MW, plus a forecast average of approximately 3,200 MW of electricity exports to neighboring systems.

That peak, driven by demand growth in the region, would set a new winter record if reached, the grid operator said. PJM’s ability to reliably serve winter demand “will depend on continued preparation and strong generator performance,” the grid operator said. “This is especially important in areas where transmission constraints limit the ability to deliver electricity from other parts of the system,” particularly including some zones in Maryland, Virginia and the District of Columbia.

ReliabilityFirst, the regional entity which monitors reliability along most of the East Coast, including the PJM grid, has about 13% of its total net winter generating capacity under a cold weather constraint, NERC noted. About 12% of the total net winter capacity in the Midwest Reliability Organization region reported a generator cold weather constraint, according to the report. In the Northeast Power Coordinating Council territory, about 4% of the total net winter capacity reported a generator cold weather constraint.

"Our goal is to coordinate operations with the generation and transmission owners and proactively manage the system to perform at high levels when consumers need energy the most," Michael Bryson, PJM senior vice president of operations, said in a statement.

#5775. Permitting bill advances in Senate, but election results cast doubt on its future

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“Senate passage is possible, but enactment remains unlikely without a clear House strategy,” Jefferies analysts said in a research note.

The cloture vote to advance the bill received 73 yesses, 25 nos and two abstentions. Among the nos were Sen. Cory Booker, D-N.J.; Sen. Adam Schiff, D-Calif.; Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vermont; and Sen. Jeff Merkley, D-Ore.

According to Politico, Schiff said he was voting no following President Donald Trump’s comment Monday that he supports the bill but “will not approve wind turbines,” adding, “The bill hasn’t been fully negotiated.”

“He has not changed course in any way when it comes to renewable energy, and any wiggle room left in the bill to prioritize oil and gas over renewables he’ll make full use of,” Schiff told Politico.

In a Monday release, Merkley said he “strongly supports” the parts of the bill that seek to increase transmission and clean energy, and ensure fair cost allocation for data centers, but said the legislation has “features that merit careful analysis” and “robust consideration of the bill should be a priority in the new Congress.”

“At the demand of the fossil fuel titans, this bill also accelerates the extraction and burning of fossil fuels,” Merkley said. “This bill could make it easier to build more data centers powered by ‘behind the meter’ natural gas plants. And it impacts the sovereignty and rights of Tribes.”

Sen. Catherine Cortez Masto, D-Nev., voted to advance the bill but warned in a Monday release that tribes in Nevada still have concerns regarding the legislation’s changes to the National Historic Preservation Act, and if those aren’t struck from the bill, she will not vote for final passage.

“The manager’s amendment to BAAJA does not adequately address the serious concerns raised by Tribal Nations across the country,” a coalition of tribal organizations said in a Sunday release.

In a research note sent Monday following the vote, Jefferies analysts said the manager’s amendment “largely preserves” the bill’s original framework “but is incrementally better for transmission, offshore-wind projects, nuclear, and non-RTO utilities, and worse for large BTM gas configurations, incumbent RTO utilities and projects relying on strong permit certainty.”

“Senate passage is possible, but enactment remains unlikely without a clear House strategy,” the note said. “The irony is that the bipartisan Senate coalition has made meaningful progress just as the opportunity to convert that progress into law is narrowing. House Democratic leaders argue that the Senate bill falls short, while Republican support for accepting the Senate product also appears uncertain.”

The analysts said the bill could see more revisions to reflect House concerns.

Some of those concerns were voiced by Rep. Adelita Grijalva, D-Ariz, who was reelected last week.

“Just one week after voters resoundingly rejected the Trump agenda, Congress should not be rushing to pass a bill that gives him even more unilateral authority to bypass our laws,” she said in a Monday statement. “I am a hard NO on BAAJA.”

On Sunday, the Sierra Club said in a release that the manager’s amendment was released amid a “continued rush to hold a vote during the lame duck session,” adding that while the amendment “partially addressed” the group’s previously outlined concerns, “major substantive issues remain.”

Former FERC Chairman Mark Christie said in a Monday LinkedIn post that the manager’s amendment had not “at all” addressed two of his three major concerns with the legislation.

“The ‘public interest’ standard in the bill remains a blank check for FERC to override the states and order trillions of dollars in new transmission built to fit the policies of a President or more likely, special interest lobbying of RTOs,” Christie said.

He added that the legislation doesn’t give states authority over cost allocation for large transmission projects “that will cost their consumers tons of money.”

“The transmission provisions are a DC lobbyists' dream list,” Christie said. “Massive expansion of federal power that will cost consumers literally trillions of dollars.”

Paul Cicio, CEO of the trade association Industrial Energy Consumers of America and chair of the Electricity Transmission Competition Coalition, criticized the bill in a Monday LinkedIn post, saying it “fails to unleash transmission competition and deliver cost savings to ratepayers.”

“Without competition, a monopoly utility has zero incentive to reduce costs,” he said. “Section 2105 codifies existing exceptions to competition that are used by the monopoly utilities to evade competition.”

Cicio also objected to a change the manager’s amendment made to Section 2104 of the bill, saying it “now gives FERC the ability to dismiss a state's request to investigate actions by a utility to evade competition and actions that would harm consumers.”

Editor’s note: This story has been updated to include additional reporting from Politico about the bill’s ongoing negotiations.
